5. User Accounts in Extra ID
Hello and welcome to User Accounts and enter ID before user can access Azure resources that user needs
in Azure User Account.
The user account is what's used to authenticate during the user sign in process.
Microsoft Entra ID actually supports three different types of user accounts cloud identities, directory
synchronized identities, and guest users.
A cloud identity is a user account that's defined only in entra ID basically, it's a cloud only user.
Cloud identities can be used for user accounts that are defined not only in your entra ID organization,
but also for user accounts that are in external entra ID instances.
Directory synchronized identities are defined in an on prem Active Directory.
So basically you get a synchronized identity when a user account is synchronized from the on prem Active
Directory into entra ID via something like interconnect or intra cloud sync.
It's important to note that the source for a directory synchronized identity is the on prem ad.
Then you have the guest user.
Guest user accounts, as you would expect, are defined outside of Azure.
Examples of a guest user would be Microsoft accounts, maybe like an Xbox Live account.
The source for a guest user is the invited user, so you send an invite to a user, they accept the
invite and then they become a guest user.
Basically, you use guest user accounts when you have external vendors or outside contractors that need
access to resources within your Azure environment.
Now let's talk a little bit about managing user accounts.
And more specifically, I really just want to focus on cloud identities.
There are several different ways you can add cloud identity user accounts to your intra ID.
Generally speaking, you're often going to do this through the intra admin center.
You would typically do this for your day to day a one off user additions.
You can also use the Microsoft 365 admin center to add user accounts.
You can use the Microsoft Intune Admin Console, the Azure CLI, or even PowerShell.
The image that you see on your screen shows what the new user page looks like in the intra admin center.
You'll notice you can create a user within the organization or invite a guest user.
I should note that any new user account has to have a display name and an associated user account name.
The display name is typically the first and last name as they would be displayed in the directory.
The user account name would simply be something like.
For example, T Mitchell at Surf City, Boats.com that's the UPN, basically.
Organizations will also include information in the user account profile like the job title for the user,
the email address, the contact email address, and other details to fill out the profile.
The key takeaway here is that Microsoft Entra ID supports three different types of user accounts cloud
identities, directory synchronized identities, and guest users.
User accounts with a cloud identity are defined only in entra ID, and these types of accounts are users
who are managed as part of the organization directory.
Synchronized identities are user accounts that are defined in an on prem active Directory, and then
synchronized into entra ID via something like interconnect.
The source for these accounts is the on prem Windows Server Active Directory.
Guest accounts are exactly what they sound like guest users that are defined outside of Azure.
These accounts are used when you have external vendors or contractors that need to access your Azure
resources.

9. Group accounts in Azure
Hello and welcome to Group Accounts.
Azure Active Directory allows you to define two different types of group accounts.
These are security groups and Microsoft 365 groups.
Security groups are used to manage user and computer access to resources for a group of users or devices.
In other words, you might create a security group called Finance App users for users that need access
to a particular finance app.
You then grant the Finance app users group permissions to the app.
This would in turn grant all users within the group the access they need.
Microsoft 365 groups, however, are used to facilitate collaboration opportunities.
Members of a microsoft 365 group will often have access to things like a shared mailbox or a shared
calendar files, a SharePoint site, etc..
The image on your screen shows a list of groups in the Azure portal.
Microsoft recommends using security groups to grant permissions to a resource for all group members
at the same time, rather than adding permissions for individual users in a one off fashion.
Microsoft 365 groups should be used to enable group access for guest users outside your Azure ad organization.
You should also keep in mind that security groups can only be created by an Azure ad administrator.
That said, both normal users and Azure ad admins can use Microsoft 365 groups.
And the last concept I want to touch on here is how users can be added to groups.
And by.
By that I mean when you create a group, you'll have a few different options for how users get added
to the group.
You'll see an assigned option, a dynamic user option, and a dynamic device option.
Selecting the assigned option allows you to manually specify which users or devices should be members
of the group.
This is typically how you create groups.
That said, choosing dynamic user allows you to define dynamic membership rules that will automatically
or dynamically add and remove group members.
So for example, if you create a marketing group, you can define a rule that dynamically adds all users
with marketing in their department attribute within their user account.
If a users attribute changes, the user is dynamically removed from the group.
Dynamic Device works in the same fashion as the dynamic user option, except it applies to devices,
not users.
It also only works with security groups and not Microsoft 365 groups.
So when sitting the exam, keep in mind or really remember that security groups are used to manage member
and computer access to shared resources for a group of users or devices.
While Microsoft 365 groups facilitate collaboration opportunities, the assigned option allows you to
manually specify which users or devices should be members of the group.
While the dynamic user option allows you to define dynamic membership rules that will automatically
add and remove group members.
Dynamic device works the same way as dynamic user, except that it only applies to devices and only
works with security groups.

11. Bulk User Accounts in Azure
Hello and welcome to Bulk User Accounts.
Azure AD allows you to perform a few different bulk operations with user accounts, including bulk creation
and deletion.
These bulk operations are typically performed in the Azure Portal.
However, you can also use Azure PowerShell to bulk upload user accounts.
The image on your screen shows the bulk create user option in the Azure ad portal.
Now let's talk a little bit about how this CSV template is structured.
The rows in the downloaded CSV template will include the version number, row, the column headings
row, and the examples row.
The version number must be included when you re upload your completed CSV.
The column headings row will include things like the name, the username, password, those type of
things.
The examples row provides an example of user with the accepted values for each column.
You'll have to remove the examples row and replace it with your own user entries before you upload your
completed CSV.
Now, it's important to note that the first two rows of the template should not be removed or modified
if they are.
The upload is not going to be processed.
The required columns are noted as such and are typically listed first.
Any columns that you add to the CSV are going to be ignored during upload, so don't add any new columns
that aren't already there.
And then you also want to ensure that the values in the initial password will comply with the currently
active password policy.
So you don't want to use simple passwords in your CSV.
If you have a complex password policy.
Now, it's important to note that only global admins and user administrators can create and delete user
accounts in the Azure Portal to bulk create or delete users.
The admin needs to fill out that CSV template and it's going to include the data for each of the user
accounts that are being created or deleted.
Now, when creating user accounts, think carefully about naming conventions and ways that you can minimize
errors.
For example, think about what naming conventions and processes are required by your organization.
Establishing a proper naming convention for your user accounts can vastly simplify the bulk Create process
by reducing areas of uniqueness in the CSV file itself.
You'll also want to think about initial passwords more specifically, determine how you're going to
securely notify new users about their passwords.
Organizations will often generate random passwords for new users and then email them to the new users
or to their managers.
To minimize errors.
You should review and address any errors during the bulk process by downloading the results file on
the bulk operation results page in the Azure Portal.
This results file will include the reason for each error that's encountered during a bulk upload.
When sitting the exam, remember that Azure ad supports several different bulk operations, including
bulk create and bulk delete.
The most common approach for these operations is to use the Azure portal, but you can use Azure PowerShell
to bulk upload user accounts.
You should also remember that only the global admin or user administrators have the ability to create
and delete user accounts in the Azure Portal.
And also remember that you use a CSV file to perform your bulk operations.

13. Guest Users / Azure AD B2B
Hello and welcome to Azure Active Directory.
B to B.
In this lesson, we're going to take a look at what Azure Active Directory B to B is and what it offers.
Now Azure Active Directory B to B is also referred to as business to business collaboration.
It's an offering that you use when you need to share your applications and services with guest users
from another organization.
By leveraging Azure Ad B to B, you can share these applications and services while still retaining
control over your data.
And what's even better is that the organizations you wish to share access with don't even need to have
an Azure Active Directory or really even an I.T department at all.
This is because of the easy invitation and redemption process.
That Azure ad B to B uses when you use Azure ADB to B to grant access to external users, the partner
organization uses their own identity management solution so you don't have to deal with any administrative
overhead that would otherwise be necessary to support the external users.
There's no need to manage external accounts or external passwords, nor is there any need to synchronize
accounts or manage account lifecycles to grant access to your resources.
Using Azure AD B to B, you simply send an invite to your guest user via email.
The guest user then redeems the invite and follows a few easy redemption steps to sign in.
If the guest user doesn't have an existing Microsoft account or Azure ad account, one is created for
them once they redeem the invitation and once your guest users are added to your directory, you can
use authorization policies to protect your content.
Now you can also add guest users to your Azure AD through the Azure ad portal, provided you're an administrator,
the process for creating a new guest user is quite similar to the process you would follow for adding
a new internal user.
Once you've added the new guest user in Azure ad that guest user receives an invitation, then that
allows them to sign in to their access panel.
You can then assign your guest user or users to applications and groups just like any other internal
user.
You can even allow application owners and group owners in your organization to manage their own guest
users.
What this does is reduce the management overhead that you as an administrator have to deal with, although
you don't necessarily need a paid Azure ad addition to use B2B for some resources, you should be aware
of how the licensing works.
Although guest users can use Free Azure ad features without any additional licensing requirements.
If there are paid Azure ad features that are required, you can invite up to five guest users for each
paid Azure ad addition license that you own.
So, for example, what this means is that if you're only using the free edition of Azure ad, you can
invite up to five guest users for every free Azure ad license that you have.
So if you have 100 free Azure ad licenses, that's 500 users, 500 guest users, I should say.
Now, those invited users will only have access to features that are included in the free edition of
Azure.
AD If you're using Azure ADP one licenses, you can invite up to five guest users for every P one license
you have, and those users can use those P one capabilities.
To read more about Azure ad B to B licensing, you can visit the URL that you see on your screen.


14. Administrative Units in Microsoft Azure
Welcome to administrative units.
When designing a strategy for managing identities and governance in Microsoft Azure, it's often helpful
to restrict administrative scopes by using what's called an administrative unit.
Now, what an administrative unit is, is an Azure ad resource that can be a container for other Azure
ad resources that may be in a case.
Administrative units allow you to essentially divvy up roles and responsibilities when you have several
independent divisions, for example.
That said, I should point out that an administrative unit can only contain users, groups or devices.
Now, because administrative units can be used to restrict permissions to any portion of your organization
that you wish you might, for example, use administrative units to delegate the helpdesk administrator
role to regional support specialists.
So in other words, you might use an administrative unit to delegate the German support specialists
to the German office while delegating the American support specialists to the US office.
This would ensure that only the proper support specialists can manage the users in their associated
region or office.
It should be noted, however, that a user can be a member of multiple administrative units if necessary.
So keep that in mind when working with administrative units, think about how you might need to implement
those administrative units within your organization.
For example, think about what tools you'll need to use to manage those administrative units.
For example, you can use the Azure Portal PowerShell and even Microsoft Graph.
You'll also want to think about role requirements.
In other words, you should plan your administrative unit design so that it's based on role privileges.
Keeping in mind that in the Azure Portal, only global admins and privileged role administrators can
manage administrative units.
And lastly, you should think about the scope of your planned administrative units, because the scope
of an administrative unit applies only to management permissions.
This means that members and admins of an administrative unit still have their default user permissions
that will allow them to browse other users, groups or resources outside of their associated administrative
unit.
So remember, when you're designing a strategy for managing identities and governance in Microsoft Azure,
you can use administrative units to restrict administrative scopes, because what an administrative
unit does is provide an Azure ad resource that can be a container for other Azure ad resources.
This allows you to use them to divvy up roles and responsibilities when you have several independent
divisions.
You'll also want to remember that an administrative unit can only contain users, groups or devices.

16. Managing Device Identities in Azure AD
Hello and welcome to Managing Device Identities in Azure AD.
If you're preparing for this exam, you probably already realize that Azure ad is Microsoft's cloud
solution for centrally managing device identities along with user identities.
Now, as you can see here on the screen, when we're talking about the devices, the devices overview
page displays the number of total devices in your environment along with stale devices, non compliant
devices and even unmanaged devices.
You can also access Intune conditional access, bitlocker keys and do some basic monitoring through
the links that are available.
Clicking all devices in the left pane allows you to see devices that have been joined or registered
in Azure AD and those that have been deployed via Windows autopilot.
It also shows or really allows you to view printers that use universal print from the all devices page.
You can enable, disable, delete and manage your devices.
Now, I should mention, however, that the management options for printers and windows autopilot devices
are rather limited in Azure AD So basically this means that they should be managed from their own admin
interfaces.
Other tasks that you can complete from within the all devices page include configuring device identity
settings, enabling or disabling enterprise, state roaming, reviewing device related audit logs,
and even downloading devices to enable or disable devices.
You can use either the toolbar on the All Devices page.
After you select a device you want to manage, or you can use the toolbar after you drill down for a
specific device.
It's important to note that you need to be a global administrator into an administrator or cloud device
administrator in Azure AD if you wish to enable or disable devices.
And speaking of disabling devices, when you do disable a device, it can no longer authenticate via
Azure AD.
So what this means is that it's prevented from accessing any Azure AD resources that are protected by
device based conditional access and from using Windows.
Hello for business credentials, you can delete Azure ad devices in the same manner as enabling or disabling
them.
You can use either the toolbar on the all devices page after you select the device that you want to
manage.
Or you can use the toolbar after you've drilled down to a specific device.
To delete a device, you must be a cloud device administrator into an administrator.
Windows 365, Administrator or global administrator in Azure AD.
Deleting a device prevents it from accessing Azure ad resources and removes all details about that device.
For example, Bitlocker keys for Windows device will go away when the device is deleted.
I should point out that device deletion is a non recoverable action.
It means it can't be undone, so be careful with it.
I should also mention that printers and windows autopilot devices cannot be deleted in Azure ad from
the all Devices page.
You can also view and copy device IDs and bitlocker keys.
I do want to point out, though, that in order to view or copy bitlocker keys, you need to either
own the device in question or have one of the roles that you see on your screen here.
If you notice here in this screenshot, there's a download Devices option.
If you're a global reader, cloud device administrator, Intune, administrator or global administrator,
you can use the download Devices Option to export a CSV file that lists all of your devices.
You can use different filters to specify which devices to list.
The CSV that's provided is going to include lots of different device identity attributes, including
things like account enabled, display named is managed object ID and a host of others.
Audit logs can also be accessed via the All devices page.
These logs will include activities that are triggered by the device registration service and by users.
Activities that are audited will include, for example, device creation, adding owners and users on
devices, changes made to device settings and device operations like deleting or updating devices.
The default audit log view shows the time and date of the occurrence, the targets, the initiator or
actor of an activity and the activity itself.
That said, you can customize the list by selecting columns in the toolbar.
You can also search for specific entries.
So as you can see here, the All Devices page really is the, I guess, central location, if you will,
for managing device identities in Azure AD.

17. Managing Device Settings in Azure AD
Hello and welcome to Managing Device Settings in Azure AD Before you can manage device identities in
Azure, they must first be either registered or joined to Azure Active Directory to control the process
of registration and device.
Join you use device settings in the Azure Portal.
Before we get into the device settings that are available, I do need to point out that you have to
have a global administrator, a cloud device administrator, a global reader, or a directory reader
role in order to manage device settings.
If you aren't assigned one of these roles, you cannot view nor manage device settings in the Azure
Portal.
The image on your screen shows what device settings looks like.
Let's go through each of these settings so you know what they do.
The users may join devices to Azure.
Ad setting allows you to choose which users can register their devices as Azure AD joined devices.
The default option for this setting is all meaning all users in Azure AD can register their own devices
as Azure ad joint devices.
It's important to note that this setting only applies to Azure AD join on Windows ten or newer.
Additionally, it does not apply to hybrid Azure AD joint devices.
Azure AD joined VMS and Azure NOR Azure AD joined devices that use Windows Autopilot self deployment
mode because these methods all work in a user list context.
Additional local administrators on Azure ADD joined devices allows you to select the users who are granted
local admin rights on a device.
The users you select here are added to the device administrators role in Azure AD.
This option is only available though through products like Azure AD Premium and enterprise mobility
and security.
I should also mention here that since global administrators in Azure AD and device owners are granted
local administrator rights by default, they don't need to be specified here.
And now here we have users may register their devices with Azure AD.
This setting is designed to allow users to register their personal Windows ten or later devices iOS
devices, Android devices, and Mac OS devices with Azure AD.
If you set this option to none, devices won't be allowed to register with Azure Active Directory.
It's important to note that enrollment with Microsoft Intune or with mobile device management for Microsoft
365 they both require registration.
That said, if either of these services is configured, the all option here will be selected and none
won't even be available.
Require multi factor authentication to join devices allows you to force users to complete multi factor
authentication before joining or registering their devices to Azure AD.
This provides an added layer of security.
That said, the default setting here is no.
However, Microsoft does recommend setting this to yes in a production environment for that added security
I mentioned.
I should also mention that this setting only applies to devices that are either Azure ad Joint or Azure
AD registered.
It doesn't apply to hybrid Azure AD joint Devices, Azure AD joint VMs and Azure nor Azure ad joint
devices that use Windows Autopilot, self deployment mode.
If you plan to use a conditional access policy to require multi factor authentication, you need to
turn this option off.
And then lastly here, we have maximum number of devices.
This option allows you to specify the maximum number of Azure ad joint or Azure ad registered devices
that a user can have in Azure Active Directory.
The default value here is 50.
So if a user reaches 50 devices, they won't be able to add any more devices until one or more of the
existing devices is removed.
That said, you can modify this value up or down.
The max value is 100.
However, you can also use unlimited to enforce no limits other than existing quotas.
This setting applies to devices that are either Azure AD joined or Azure AD registered, but does not
apply to hybrid Azure AD joined devices.
So really before setting the exam, what you want to do is be sure that you familiarize yourself with
each of these device settings and what they do, because you may be asked to perform certain device
centric tasks that require knowledge of these settings.

19. Azure Regions and Region Pairs
Hello and welcome to Azure Regions and Region Pairs.
Microsoft Azure is made up of data centers that are located all around the world.
A region in this context refers to a specific geographical location that contains at least one data
center.
That being the case, Azure is available in over 60 regions in 140 countries.
Data centers in Azure are physically located close to one another and are connected together via a low
latency network.
A few examples of regions would be things like East US, Canada, Central West Europe, Australia,
East and Japan West.
As your regions allow you to host solutions in geographies that are closest to your end users.
This makes them useful for preserving data, residency and even adhering to geography based compliance
policies.
When discussing Azure regions, you'll sometimes hear the term regional pair or region pair.
This term refers to Azure regions, which are paired with another region within the same geography.
An example of a regional pair in Asia would be East Asia and Southeast Asia, while in North America
an example would be East US and West US.
Regional pairs allow you to build solutions that are essentially always on.
In other words, since Azure tries to maintain at least 300 miles of separation between the data centers
in a regional pair, the likelihood of natural disasters, civil unrest, power outages, or even physical
network outages affecting both regions at once is minimized.
For example, during a broad outage, Microsoft is going to prioritize the recovery of one region out
of every pair.
This means that any application that's deployed across paired regions will be guaranteed to have one
of its regions recovered with priority.
I should also mention that planned Azure system updates get rolled out to paired regions in a sequential
fashion, meaning you'll never see updates rolled out to both regions and the pair at the same time.
This minimizes downtime and reduces bugs and even minimizes logical failures in the rare event of a
bad update.
Now, I do want to point out that while there are lots of different regions available when deploying
resources, some Azure services or even some VM features are available only in certain regions.
For example, certain VM sizes or storage types might only be available in specific regions.
It's also important to note that some global Azure services don't require you to select a region.
These services include Azure Active Directory, Microsoft Azure Traffic Manager, and Azure DNS.
So when you sit the exam, it's important to note that or really I should say it's important to remember
that Microsoft Azure is made up of data centers that are located all around the world.
A region refers to a specific geographical location that contains at least one data center.
Regions are useful for preserving data residency and adhering to geography based compliance policies
while region pairs allow you to deploy solutions that are protected against broad outages like natural
disasters, civil unrest, power outages and physical network outages.


20. Azure Subscriptions
Hello and welcome to Understanding Azure Subscriptions.
Now, before you can do anything in Azure, you have to sign up for an Azure subscription.
Now what a subscription does is provide you with authenticated and authorized access to the different
Azure products and services that are available.
And of course, once you have your Azure subscription provisioned, you can then provision resources
within it, you can deploy VMS, virtual networks, etc..
One of the things to remember about an Azure subscription is that what a subscription is really is a
logical unit of Azure services.
And that logical unit of Azure services links back to an Azure account.
That Azure account is an identity in Azure Active Directory or it can be in a directory that Azure AD
trusts.
Generally speaking though, it links back to an Azure Active Directory or Azure AD an account can actually
have one subscription or it can have multiple subscriptions, each having their own different billing
models.
And each of those subscriptions can also have their own access management policies applied.
Essentially what you do with an Azure subscription is you use it to define boundaries around Azure products
and services.
There's actually two types of boundaries that Azure Subscriptions function as billing, boundary and
access control.
Now, when used as a billing boundary and Azure subscription determines how the Azure account is billed
for using Azure, for example, you can create multiple subscriptions for different types of billing
requirements.
And what'll happen is Azure will generate separate billing reports and invoices for each of those subscriptions,
and that allows you to better organize and manage costs.
For example, if you have multiple departments within a large organization, you could in theory have
each department with its own subscription so that billing could be managed by department.
Now that said, you can also use Azure subscriptions as an access control boundary.
So what do I mean by using a subscription as an access control boundary?
Well, Microsoft Azure applies access management policies at the subscription level.
That being the case, if you create separate subscriptions, what you can do is reflect the different
organizational structure within your company and using the same department example I used when discussing
the billing boundary.
If you have different departments and you want to apply specific Azure subscription policies to each
of those departments.
You can do that by using separate subscriptions for each department.
Essentially, this billing model allows you to manage and control access to the resources that users
provisioned with specific subscriptions.
Generally speaking, you'd want to create additional subscriptions for resource or billing management
purposes.
For example, you might create additional subscriptions to separate different environments.
You might do it to separate your organizational structure.
You might do it for billing requirements, or you might do it because there are certain subscription
limits.
For example, the maximum number of express root circuits for each subscription is ten.
So if you have an environment where you need more express route circuits, then what you could do is
create multiple subscriptions to bypass that limit of ten express route circuits.
So in the case where you do have multiple subscriptions, what you can do is organize them into what
are called invoice sections.
An invoice section is a line item on your invoice, and that line item is going to show the charges
incurred for that month.
So, for example, you might need a single invoice for your entire organization, but you need to break
it out by department or team or project.
So depending on your needs, you can set up these multiple invoices within the same billing account.
And to do this, you create what are called billing profiles.
And each billing profile is going to have its own monthly invoice and payment method associated with
it.
The image on your screen shows what this type of billing structure looks like.
So when you set the exam, it's important to remember that a subscription provides you with authenticated
and authorized access to your Azure products and services, and it allows you to provision your resources.
And Azure subscription is a logical unit of Azure services that links typically to an Azure account,
which is an identity in Azure Active Directory.
Or it can link back to a directory that Azure AD trusts.
It can also function as a billing boundary or as an access control boundary.
And if you have multiple subscriptions, you can organize them into different invoice sections by creating
billing profiles.


21. Using Management Groups in Azure
Hello, and welcome to Management Groups in Microsoft Azure.
Now, because organizations will often need to use
multiple subscriptions for various reasons, for example,
including management of access, policies, and compliance,
management groups are available in Azure.
What management groups do is provide a level of scope and
control that sits really above the subscription level, if you will.
Because of this upper level scope,
management groups are typically used as containers
to manage things like access policy and compliance
across multiple subscriptions.
It's important to note that all new subscriptions
are placed under the top level management group by default.
This top level management group is called the root group.
As a result, all subscriptions within a management group
will automatically inherit the conditions
that are applied to that management group.
I should also mention that a management group tree
can support up to six levels of depth.
The image on your screen shows what a tree might look like
and how management groups can be used
to organize subscriptions.
Notice how the organization has a single
top level management group,
and how every directory under the root group
is folded into that top level group.
Now let's talk a little bit about management group use cases.
For example, when you align your Azure subscriptions
into hierarchies and groupings that reflect
the organizational structure of the business,
you can use management groups to target policies
and spending budgets across those subscriptions.
Management groups are also often used to control
the inheritance of access and privileges
in policy definitions,
since subscriptions within a management group
will inherit the conditions applied
to that management group.
I touched on this earlier.
For example, you could apply a policy
to a management group that limits
which regions VMs can be deployed to.
Applying this policy to a management group
ensures that it's also applied to all subscriptions
and resources within that management group.
Doing this would ensure that VMs are created
only in the regions that you allow in your policy.
Organizing subscriptions into management groups
also helps organizations meet compliance rules
for individual departments and teams.
And then lastly, you can, for example,
use management groups to do cost reporting by department
or to report on budget details
across multiple subscriptions.
Management groups can be created via the Azure portal,
PowerShell, or even Azure CLI.
The image on your screen shows how you would create
a management group in the Azure portal.
Now, when you create a management group,
you need to specify a unique management group ID
and an optional display name.
The ID is used to submit commands on that management group
and cannot be changed.
The display name is optional
and you can change that at any time.
So when sitting the exam,
remember that management groups
provide a level of scope and control
that sits above the subscription level.
And also keep in mind that they're typically used
as containers to manage access policy
and compliance across multiple subscriptions.
It's also important to remember that all subscriptions
within a management group
will automatically inherit conditions
that are applied to that management group.

22. Configuring and Managing Azure Policy
Hello and welcome to Azure Policy.
Azure policy allows you to create, assign and manage policies that are used to enforce rules on resources,
so they meet corporate compliance standards and even some service level agreements.
It runs real time evaluations and it scans against those resources to make sure that they're compliant.
It also supports on demand evaluations and scans as well.
Azure policy provides built in policies that you can use right out of the box, and it offers the ability
to build out custom policies for all kinds of resources.
The policies that you create are then applied to management groups within the organization.
And by the way, those management groups I'm talking about are used to provide a level of scope and
control that sits above your subscriptions.
That means you can use them as containers to manage access policies, money and compliance across multiple
subscriptions within an organization.
Using Azure policy, you can apply multiple policies and aggregate policy states with what are called
policy initiatives.
And you do that while defining exclusion scopes when it's necessary.
As a result, you can conduct remediation on new resources as well as on existing ones.
Now let's touch on some typical use cases for Azure policy.
For example, some Azure policy use cases would include the management of deployable resources.
And what I mean by that is organizations will often use Azure policy to specify resource types that
are allowed to be deployed.
For example, an organization might use it to specify a set of VMs, SKUs that can be deployed.
So you can only deploy certain SKUs for virtual machines within the organization.
Organizations will also often use Azure policy to restrict the locations where Azure resources can be
deployed.
So, for example, an organization might specify certain geographic locations or regions where resources
can be deployed to.
In other words, maybe they lock down resource deployment to North America so only resources can be
deployed in North America and not to Europe, for example.
As your policy is also often used to enforce compliance rules and configuration options.
An example of this might be a situation where you want to use Azure policy to enforce a required tag
on resources and to define the allowed values for that tag.
And then you could also use Azure policy with the Azure backup service on VMs to run things like inventory
audits.
While policies are used to define conventions for resources, policy definitions refer to the compliance
conditions for resources and the actions that should be taken when the conditions of the policy are
met.
Taking things a step further.
Policy definitions can be grouped into what are called initiative definitions.
Now what these are.
These are used to control the scope of your policies and to evaluate the compliance of your resources.
Now, there's really four main steps when working with policy definitions.
These steps will include policy definition creation, creation of an initiative, definition, scoping
of the initiative, definition, and determining compliance.
A policy definition defines a condition to evaluate along with the actions that should be taken when
the condition is met.
When creating a policy definition, you can create your own custom definitions or you can choose from
built in definitions that are provided by Azure policy.
An example of this is a situation where you can create a policy definition to prevent VMs from, for
example, being deployed with a public IP address.
Examples of built in policy definitions include the allowed virtual machine size SKUs and allowed locations.
Definitions allowed virtual machine size SKUs is used to specify a set of VM size SKUs that your organization
can deploy while allowed locations is used to restrict the locations where resources can be deployed
to.
As I mentioned earlier, an initiative definition is a set of policy definitions that track resource
compliance state.
As your policy allows you to create your own custom initiative definitions or you can use built in definitions
provided by the service.
For example, you might use an initiative definition to ensure resources are compliant with security
regulations.
An example of a built in initiative definition would be audit machines with insecure password security
settings.
This is used to deploy an audit policy that evaluates affected resources to check for insecure password
security settings.
Now, because Azure policy allows you to control how your initiative definitions are applied to resources
in your organization, you can limit the scope of an initiative definition to specific management groups,
subscriptions, or resource groups.
In other words, the scope is going to determine what resources or grouping of resources are affected
by the conditions of the policies you configure.
The image on your screen shows how to configure scope assignment.
And then once you've assigned an initiative definition, you can evaluate the state of compliance for
resources affected by that definition.
This, of course, only applies to resources, those that haven't been exempted via exclusions that
you've set up.
The screenshot you're looking at now shows how you can use the compliance feature to look for non compliant
initiatives, policies and resources.
Note that the compliance state result is either compliance or non compliant.
So when you set the exam.
Be sure to remember that policies are used to define conventions for resources.
Policy definitions refer to the compliance conditions for resource, along with the actions that should
be taken when the conditions of the policy are met.
Policy definitions can be grouped into an initiative definition which is used to control the scope of
your policies and to evaluate the compliance of your resources.

24. Resource Locks in Azure
Hello and welcome to Resource Locks.
In this lesson, you'll learn what resource locks are and why you use them.
Microsoft Azure offers you the ability to lock Azure subscriptions, resource groups and even individual
resources so that they cannot be accidentally deleted or modified.
When viewed in the Azure portal.
These locks are called delete and read only, and they both override any user permissions that might
apply to the resources you lock with them.
In the command line, however, the locks are called cannot delete and read only.
Keep that in mind.
The delete lock and cannot delete lock are both the same lock.
Just different names.
It's a weird naming anomaly, but they both refer to the same lock.
The cannot delete lock allows you to ensure authorized users can read and modify a resource but not
delete it.
While read only allows users to read a resource but not delete or update it.
Basically, when you apply the read only lock, it has a similar effect of restricting authorized users
to the permissions that the reader role provides.
I should mention that locks are different from roll based access control because locks are used to apply
restrictions across all users and roles.
The image on your screen shows how and where to set locks for a particular resource in this particular
case, you see a VM being locked.
Now, I should mention here that a lock applied at a parent scope will apply to all resources within
that scope as well.
So in other words, locks are inherited.
Even resources that are added after a lock has been applied to the parent scope.
I should also emphasize that the most restrictive lock in the inheritance will take precedence.
Inheritance is important to understand because if you have a delete lock set, let's say on a resource,
and then try to delete the resource group containing that locked resource, the whole delete operation
is going to be blocked, which makes sense.
It's important to note that locks only apply to what are called control plane operations.
They don't apply to data plane operations.
So in other words, a lock will protect a resource from changes, but it won't restrict how that resource
performs its functions.
So, for example, if you apply a read only lock to a SQL database logical server, the lock is going
to prevent someone from deleting or modifying the logical server itself, but it will still allow you
to do things like create, update and delete data within the server database because locks only affect
control plane operations, applying them can sometimes produce unexpected results.
For example, if you apply a read only lock on a storage account, what that's going to do is prevent
users from listing the account keys for that storage account.
That's because a post request handles the Azure storage list keys operation in order to protect access
to those account keys.
Applying a read only lock on a storage account will also prevent the creation of a blob container,
since the container is an object within the storage account.
That said, neither lock type applied to a storage account will protect the data within the account
from deletion or modification.
This includes data in blobs, queues, tables and files.
So who exactly can create or delete locks?
Well, before you can create or delete management locks, you need access to the Microsoft authorization
or Microsoft authorization Locks actions.
So what this means is that the only the owner and the user access administrator built in roles can create
and delete management locks unless you create a custom role with those required permissions.
So when you set the exam, there's a there's a couple of things you want to remember about locks.
You want to make sure that you remember that Microsoft Azure offers the ability to lock not only Azure
subscriptions, but also resource groups and even individual resources using locks.
This prevents them from being accidentally deleted or modified.
The locks themselves are called delete or cannot delete depending on where you're viewing them from
and read only.
They both override user permissions that might apply to the resources you lock with them.
The delete lock allows you to ensure that authorized users can read and modify a resource but not delete
it.
While the read only lock allows you to read a resource but not delete or update it.
You should also remember that locks are different from our back because locks are used to apply restrictions
across all users and roles, and they're inherited from parent scopes.
And then lastly, remember, that locks only apply to control plane operations.

28. Resource Tagging in Azure
Hello and welcome to Resource Tagging in Microsoft Azure.
When working in Azure, you can apply tags that you define to each of your Azure resources.
These tags are used to logically organize your resources by categories that you define.
Organizations will often tag resources because doing so makes it easier to sort them, search for them,
manage them, and even perform analysis on them.
When you create a tag, the tag is going to consist of a name and a value.
So, for example, you could name the tag environment and set the value to production.
You could then apply this tag to resources in your production environment.
The image on your screen shows how to add tags for a resource group in the Azure portal.
Now, it's important to note that every resource tag has a name and a value.
The name remains consistent for all resources that have the tag applied, while the tag value can be
selected from a defined set of values or unique for a specific resource instance.
It should also be pointed out that tags that are applied to a resource group are not inherited by the
resources within the resource group.
When using tags, keep in mind that they allow you to search for resources in your subscription by searching
for the tag name and value, they allow you to find related resources, even if they exist in other
resource groups, as long as they all have the same tag name or tag value.
I should also mention that organizations will often group resources by cost, center or department in
order to break out the cost for billing back to those cost centers or departments.

30. Managing Azure Resource Groups
The fact that you’re preparing for this exam means you probably already know what a resource group is, but just to refresh - a resource group is a container in Azure that holds the resources that you deploy. If we’re talking best practices, a resource group will hold all the resources for a specific solution that you deploy. In other words, all resources deployed to a specific resource group should ideally share the same lifecycle. However, you can really group your resources into any number of resource groups, however you want.

32. Self-Service Password Reset in Azure
Hello and welcome to self service Password Reset.
Over the next few minutes you'll learn how self service password reset works in Azure Active Directory.
Now, once a user logs in to Azure ad they can change their password if they so desire.
However, if that user's password is forgotten or if it expires at a point when the user isn't logged
in, they'll need to reset that password.
Self service Password reset is designed for exactly these situations.
Using self service password reset.
A user can reset their password via a web browser or from a windows sign in screen.
In doing so, they can regain access to things like Azure, Microsoft 365 or any other application that
uses Azure AD for authentication.
When a user initiates a password reset from either the password reset portal or by clicking the can't
access your account link on a sign in page.
The password reset portal requires the user to enter their username and to complete a CAPTCHA.
This is done to filter out bots.
The user then enters a code or answers a security question or security questions in order to be authenticated.
Once authenticated, the user can enter a new password and confirm it.
Lastly, a notification gets sent to the user to confirm the password reset.
Now, to facilitate the authentication of a user during a password reset, a microsoft Azure offers
a six different ways to authenticate the reset request.
The administrator gets to choose which of these methods go into play when configuring self service password
reset.
When configuring self service password reset, you should enable at least two of the methods so that
users can choose the ones that they can use more easily.
The authentication methods that are available include mobile app notification, a mobile app code,
email, mobile phone, office, phone and security questions.
Each of these is highlighted in detail on the table on your screen.
It's important to note that in the Free and Trial Azure ad organizations, the phone call options are
not available.
Now, when you're configuring self service password reset, you'll need to specify the minimum number
of authentication methods that your users will be required to set up.
Now, there's only two options for this setting, one or two.
So for example, you might decide to configure SPR, which is self service password reset by enabling
the mobile app code email and security questions authentication methods while specifying a minimum of
two methods.
Doing this would allow end users to choose the two methods that they prefer.
They'd be required to set up at least two of the three methods.
I should point out that if you opt to enable the security question method, you can specify a minimum
number of questions that the user will need to set up in order to use this method.
I should also point out that you can also specify a minimum number of questions that must be answered
in order to allow a password reset to proceed.
Once a user registers the required information for the minimum number of authentication methods that
you've specified that users then consider to be registered for SPR.
In addition to enabling at least two authentication methods, Microsoft also recommends using either
the mobile app notification or mobile app code as the primary method, while also enabling the email
or office phone methods.
In case your users don't have mobile devices for some reason.
It's important to note that although the mobile phone method is available, Microsoft actually doesn't
recommend it, since it's possible to send fraudulent SMS messages.
The security question option is the least recommended method.
This is because the answers to these kinds of questions are often known by other people.
That being the case, Microsoft recommends only using this method in combination with at least one other
method.
Now, I do want to point out here that a strong two method authentication policy is always applied to
accounts with an administrator role, regardless of how you configure PR for the other users in the
organization.
It's also important to keep in mind that the security questions option is not available to accounts
that are associated with an administrator role.
Now, during SPR configuration, you can specify how users get notified of password changes.
You have two options for this.
You can notify users on password resets and you can notify all admins when other admins reset their
password.
Choosing.
Notify users on password resets causes the user who resets their own password to be notified of that
reset at their primary and secondary email addresses.
This allows the user to take mitigation steps in the event the password change happened maliciously
choosing notify all admins when other admins reset their password.
Causes all administrators to be notified when another administrator resets their password.
As far as licensing requirements go, password reset capabilities will be dependent on the addition
of Azure ad you're using.
The additions of Azure ad include things like Azure ad free premium P one, P two and the versions that
are included with Microsoft 365 apps.
Any user who is signed in can change their password regardless of what addition of Azure AD is in play.
However, if a user is not signed in and they've forgotten their password or maybe their password is
expired, they can use SPR to reset their password.
As long as you're using Azure AD premium P one or P two.
This functionality is also available in Microsoft 365 apps for business and in Microsoft 365.
If you're in a hybrid environment where there's an Active Directory on prem and Azure ad in the cloud,
password changes in the cloud have to be written back to the on prem ad.
This is called Password write Back and it's only available in Azure AD premium P one or P two and in
Microsoft 365 apps for business.
Now speaking of password, write back deployment of ssps R with password write back is done through
either Azure ad Connect or via Cloud sync depending on your specific needs.
That said, each option can be deployed alongside one another in different domains in order to target
different sets of users.
What this does is help existing on prem users to write back password changes, while also adding an
option for users in disconnected domains due to a company merger or split.
In other words, users from an existing on prem domain could use Azure Ad Connect, while new users
from a merger can use cloud sync in another domain.
So before you set the exam, you want to remember a couple of different things.
You want to make sure that you can remember that users can reset their passwords via a web browser or
from a Windows sign in screen.
When configuring SPR, you should enable at least two authentication methods so that users can choose
the ones that they can use more easily.
The six methods that are available include mobile app notifications, mobile app code, email, mobile
phone, office phone and security questions.
You should also remember that two method authentication is always applied to accounts with an administrator
role, regardless of how you configure SPR for the other users in the organization.
And then, of course, you should also remember what functionality in SPR is available in which edition
of Azure Active Directory.

* Configure authentication methods such as email, mobile phone, etc for SSPR.

35. Configure User Accounts for MFA
Welcome back.
In this lesson, I'm going to show you how to require two step verification for your users in Azure
Ad now you can take one of two different approaches for requiring two step verification or MFA for your
users.
Now, in both cases you need to be a global admin to implement them.
The first option for configuring MFA for your users is to enable each user individually for Azure multifactor
authentication.
When you do this, each time a user logs in, they have to perform two step verification or meet the
MFA requirements each time they sign in.
Now of course there are some exceptions to that.
For example, if they sign in from a trusted IP address that you have configured, or if a remembered
device is actually the device they're logging in on.
The second way to require MFA for your users is to set up conditional access policies.
What this does is require multifactor authentication under certain circumstances.
Now what you can't do is set up both at the same time.
If you do, what's going to happen is once you enable the multifactor authentication for a user that
would override any conditional access policies anyway.
What we're going to do here in this quick demo is walk through the process of enabling MFA for a user
directly.
Now on the screen here, you see I'm logged in to my Azure ad, what I need to do to enable MFA for
a user or for all users for that matter is to click on users under manage here.
And in the all users pane, we can see all of my users in Azure AD and we can also see an option here
for multifactor authentication.
So we'll go ahead and click on multifactor authentication here.
And then from here we're taking to our Active Directory multi-factor authentication pane here.
And from here we can see that we have MFA disabled for all of our users.
Now we can also see here that we cannot enable it for our external users.
We can only do this for our internal Davis Dexcom users here.
So if I want to enable MFA for a single user, I can simply select the user from the list by checking
the box next to the user name and then clicking enable.
And at this point I'm prompted to enable multi-factor authentication.
And then we can close the box here and then we can see multifactor is enabled.
Now, if I select Abe's account here again, we'll see.
We have three options disable, enforce and manage user settings.
We can disable MFA by clicking disable.
Now Microsoft offers the ability here to click enforce to enforce it.
But you shouldn't do this.
Microsoft's documentation actually states that a user that's enabled for MFA will automatically be switched
to enforced when that user registers for Azure MFA.
Microsoft actually says do not manually change user states to enforce.
So if you don't want people changing them to enforce, why put it here?
That's a little bit of background on the enforce option.
And then of course, we can click on manage user settings and then we can see a couple of different
options here.
Require selected users to provide contact methods.
Again, we can also delete all existing app passwords generated by the selected users and we can restore
multifactor authentication on all remember devices.
Now the option to require users to provide contact methods.
Again, what this does is force the user to complete the MFA registration process again.
Now, if we selected this option for Abe, what Abe would have to do is run through the MFA registration
process again.
Now, that being said, any non browser apps would continue to work for him as long as he has a password
set up for them.
Now, speaking of app passwords, if we selected the second option here to delete all existing app passwords,
what this does is pretty self-explanatory.
It deletes all app passwords that a user has created.
Now, what would happen here is that any non browser apps that were associated with any app passwords
that Abe created, those apps would stop working until a new app password was created.
This third option here to restore multifactor authentication on devices.
So if Abe has a phone that he always uses, he can configure that as a trusted device.
So what he could do is set that so he wouldn't have to be prompted every time he logged in for MFA.
Now, however, if his account was compromised or that trusted device was lost, we'd need a way to
remove that trusted status and require two step verification again.
That's what we would do here with this checkbox.
We would we would essentially be telling Azure ad that the user needs to perform two step verification
on that trusted device, even though it's marked as trusted.
So we'll cancel this here.
And now, if we had to enable multifactor authentication for all of our users, what we could do here,
let me disable it here for Abe.
So we have all of our users here that are disabled.
What we could do is select them all.
And then click Enable.
And then we could enable multi-factor authentication for all of our users using bulk update.
I'm not going to do it here, so I'll just hit cancel.
But it's easy enough.
That's how you would do it.
So now that I've shown you how to enable multi factor authentication for your users, both individually
and in bulk, let's call it a wrap here and I'll see you over in the next lesson.


36. Configure Fraud Alerts
Welcome back.
In this lesson, I want to touch briefly on fraud alerts in Azure MFA.
It's a pretty simple solution that allows your users to report fraudulent attempts to access resources.
Essentially, what fraud alerts do is allow users to report fraud attempts by using the mobile app or
through their phone.
Now, to turn on fraud alerts, you need to be logged in to the Azure portal as an admin.
And throughout this course I've been logged in as an admin to get to fraud alerts.
We need to browse to our Azure ad, which you can see here.
I'm logged into on our screen here and then we select MFA.
Under security here in the left pane.
Once we're in our multi-factor authentication screen under settings here, we can see the option for
fraud alert.
So we'll go ahead and select fraud alert here.
And then the screen we're presented with is pretty basic.
Like I said, this isn't a difficult setting to configure.
If we look at the screen here, we're told right out of the gate that fraud alerts allow users to report
fraud if they receive a two step verification request that they didn't initiate.
We basically have three options here.
We can allow users to submit fraud alerts.
We can automatically block users who report fraud, and then we can configure the code that the user
can use to report fraud during the initial greeting when they get the call.
So to enable our users to submit fraud alerts, we simply click the toggle to on.
Once we turn this option on, we then have the ability to automatically block users who report fraud.
Now it stands to reason that if we have a fraudulent request for two step verification, it probably
makes sense, especially in high security environments, to automatically block a user who has reported
that fraud.
However, you can turn this off if you need to.
Now, this third option here, okay, when a user receives a phone call to perform that two step verification,
the user would be prompted to press pound to confirm the sign in.
Now in order to report fraud, what the user could do is enter a code before pressing pound, and the
code that they enter is whatever you put here.
Now the code is zero by default.
But we could change this to one to whatever we want to change it to.
We can leave it at zero.
So when they receive that verification call, what they would do to submit a fraud alert is instead
of just pressing pound to confirm the sign in, they would press £0 to submit the fraud alert.
So we'll go ahead and save this.
And so now my users are allowed to submit fraud alerts in my current configuration here.
Those users are not automatically blocked.
And to submit those fraud alerts, our users would enter £0 when they receive the call to perform two
step verification.
So that is how you configure fraud alerts for your end users in Microsoft's Azure MFA.


37. Configure Bypass Options
Welcome back.
One of the exam objectives is to understand how to configure one time bypass for your users for MFA.
Now, what one time bypass is is a feature that allows a user to authenticate one time without performing
a two step verification.
So essentially it's it's a workaround that allows an MFA enabled user to log in without performing that
two step a single time.
Now that bypass obviously is temporary and the bypass is going to expire after whatever the specified
number of seconds is.
Generally speaking, you would use the one time bypass feature in situations where maybe a user's mobile
app isn't working or maybe the phone is not receiving notifications or they can't receive phone calls
for some reason.
In those cases, what you can do is use the bypass to allow that user to access whatever resource that
they're trying to access.
Now, the one time bypass only works with MFA Server, which is the on prem solution.
Now what I'm going to do here is run you through a quick demo to show you how you enable one time bypass
for MFA server.
Now on the screen here, you'll see I'm in my Davis X Azure Active Directory here.
Now what I'm going to do to enable one time bypass is browse down to MFA under security here.
Now once I'm in my multifactor authentication screen under manage MFA server, I can see here I have
a one time bypass option.
Now, before I select this, I want to clarify this is under manage MFA server.
So this is a solution for MFA server only, not the cloud based multifactor authentication.
So I'll go ahead and select one time bypass here.
And the screen here is pretty simple.
What we're going to do is allow a user to authenticate without performing two step verification.
And this ability is going to work for the number of bypass seconds that I specify here.
And then you can see here up under one time bypass.
This feature only applies to MFA server deployment.
So it tells you specifically this is only an MFA server solution.
So to configure a one time bypass for a user in our MFA server deployment, what we can do is select
the amount of time we want to give the user.
Let's say we want to give them three minutes, we could set it to 100 and 80s and then we would add
the user that we want to configure this for.
So let's assume we have an MFA server user named Lester Murphy.
We would specify his account here.
And then we would give a reason.
And then we would okay it.
Now, what this would do is give Lester Murphy three minutes to log in without needing to provide MFA
information and after three minutes this would expire and then Lester would again be prompted for MFA
if he tried to access anything via logging in.
And then of course we can cancel the action and we'll say he found his phone.
And we'll okay it.
You can see here it's removing the bypass and there you have it.
So that is how you enable and disable one time bypass for users in the MFA server option.


38. Configure Trusted IPs
Welcome back.
In this lesson, we're going to talk a little bit about trusted IPS.
Now, trusted IPS is a feature of Azure MFA.
And what you do with it is you use it to bypass two step verification for any users who sign in from
the company intranet or the company LAN.
What trusted IPS do is make life easier for your end users who are logging in from what is essentially
a trusted location, which is your corporate LAN.
Now, before we get any further, it's important to note that the trusted IPS option only works with
IPV four addresses when configuring trusted IPS bypass.
You need to understand that this feature only works from within the inside of the company intranet.
You can't be trusting public IPS from someplace on the internet.
It's not going to work.
There are two different ways that you can use to enable trusted IPS.
You can enable trusted IPS by using conditional access, or you can enable trusted IPS by using the
service settings.
Now, practically speaking, either way takes you to the service settings, and I'll show you that here.
So I'm logged in to my Davis Active Directory here.
Now, if I click on conditional access here under security.
I have an option here to configure my trusted IPS by using named locations.
Now, from named locations here, I can configure MFA.
Trusted IPS.
Selecting this here takes me out to my MFA service settings.
Now let me close this window here and we'll go back to our Azure ad.
Now if I go to my users page.
And again, go to multi factor authentication here.
Both options take me out to the MFA service page.
So to configure trusted IPS for my environment, what I need to do is get to my MFA service page via
one of the two options I just showed you.
Once I'm here, what I need to do is go to service settings and then from service settings, I have
a piece of this configuration here that covers trusted IPS.
Now I have two options here.
I can skip multifactor authentication for requests from Federated users on my intranet, and I can skip
multifactor authentication for requests coming from a specific range of IP address subnets.
Now I can configure both of these options here.
I can check the box here to skip authentication for internet users and I can add additional IP address
subnets.
Now, if I select the first option here to skip multifactor authentication for requests from the Internet,
all federated users who sign in from the corporate network are going to bypass two step verification
by using a claim that's issued by adfs.
Now remember, this is an option for federated users, so it's using Adfs.
Now, if you're going to use this option, you need to ensure that Adfs has a rule to add Internet claims
to the appropriate traffic.
Now, if you're not using Adfs, you can turn this option off and then specify a range of IP address
subnets.
Now the address is here.
Need to be entered in Cidr notation.
For example, let's say I need everyone in my intranet, which is 19216800 slash 16 to bypass MFA.
I'd enter in 192.168.0.0 slash 16 Cidr Notation.
Now we can also enter individual IP addresses here.
Let's say we need Lester to bypass MFA and Lester only when he's logging in from his workstation at
his cubicle.
What we could do is enter a specific IP address like this.
The slash 32 mask would mask the entire address, rendering it a specific IP address.
After entering any of our trusted IPS here, we would scroll down and click Save.
We close this out and then we'd now be left with a list of trusted IPS that are going to allow users
to bypass MFA whenever they log in from those IP addresses.


39. Configure Verification Methods
Welcome back.
Let's talk a little bit about verification methods when configuring multifactor authentication in Azure
Active Directory.
Now, when you configure MFA for your end users, you need to provide them with verification methods.
When your users enroll their accounts in Azure MFA, they get to choose what their preferred verification
methods are and the choices they receive are going to be dependent on what you select when you configure
your MFA service.
If we scroll down here on our screen, we can see that we have a couple of different verification options.
We have call to phone.
Text message to phone.
Notification through mobile app and verification code from mobile app or hardware token.
Now what I want to do is just briefly touch on these so you understand what each one does, and then
we'll go through how to enable and disable them, which as you can see on your screen, is probably
going to be pretty easy.
Now, the call to phone method, what this does is place an automated phone call.
It's an actual voice call that goes to the end user's telephone number.
When a user authenticates, they're going to receive that call.
When they receive that call, the user answers and then has to press the pound key in the number keypad
to authenticate.
What that does is tell Azure ad that the user has the phone that it's being called, So it's a good
authentication method.
Now the second option, which is text message to phone is probably what you're used to seeing.
Essentially when a user logs in and they're presented with an MFA challenge, the user receives a text
message with a verification code.
The user's then prompted to enter that verification code into the sign in interface that they're trying
to sign into the text message to phone.
It's also referred to as one way SMS.
The third option is notification through mobile app.
And what this does is sends a push notification to the phone or registered device for the user who's
trying to authenticate.
What happens then is that the user views the notification and then clicks a verify button in the app
to complete that verification.
An example of this would be the Microsoft Authenticator app.
I think Okta has an application too for the Okta solution.
And then the last option here is verification code from mobile app or hardware token.
Now when you use this option, the mobile app in this example, the Microsoft Authenticator app, what
it does is generates a new OAuth verification code every 30s.
Now what happens at that point is the user enters the verification code into the sign in interface.
For example, let's say the user is trying to log in to a VPN that's configured for MFA.
Once they enter their credentials, they're prompted with another box for a verification code.
What they do is they take the verification code that the authenticator app is displaying and they enter
that into the sign in interface, and then that serves as the second verification piece.
So depending on your applications and your requirements, you can use some or all of these options.
And enabling and disabling different verification methods is as simple as unchecking the boxes and checking
the boxes.
We do have to select at least one.
So I'll leave text message here and then I'll click Save.
And there you have it.
So that should give you a pretty good overview of the different verification methods that are available
in MFA and how to select them in the MFA service settings.

46. Creating and Configuring Storage Accounts
Welcome back.
In this lesson, we're going to talk about storage accounts a little bit, a storage account groups,
several different Azure storage services together in one place so you can manage them as a group.
This grouping of Azure Storage services includes blobs, files, queues and Azure tables.
When you create a storage account, any settings that you configure during or after creation are going
to apply to all services within that storage account.
I should also mention that when you delete a storage account, any data within it is also deleted.
When you create a storage account, you're going to need to choose what kind of storage account you
want to use.
This is because the storage account kind determines what services can be hosted within it, as well
as the pricing of those services.
There's actually three kinds of storage accounts.
You have Storage V two or otherwise known as General purpose V two.
You have storage or general purpose V one and you have blob storage.
Storage V two supports all storage types and all of the latest features while storage or general purpose
V one is essentially at this point a legacy kind of storage account that does support all storage types,
but it may no longer support all features.
Blob storage is also now a legacy storage account and it only supports block blobs and append blobs.
At this point, Microsoft recommends that you use the Storage V two or general purpose v two option
for all new storage accounts.
As you can see here in the screenshot, I provided the settings that you configure for storage accounts
include things like the Azure subscription that's going to host the storage accounts, the data center
or location where the storage account is going to live.
Performance options are standard and premium Standard allows you to host blob file queue and table storage
and it uses magnetic disk drives, while premium provides more options, including storing unstructured
object data as block blobs or pen blobs and specialized file storage used to store and create premium
file shares.
Premium storage accounts use solid state drives.
Replication specifies how Azure is going to make copies of your data to protect it from hardware failures
or natural disasters.
The minimum replication is called locally redundant storage or loss, and it protects data against hardware
failures within a data center.
But it doesn't protect it from a data center wide outage.
Other options provide more robust replication.
For example, geo redundant storage or GRC provides replication at different data centers around the
globe.
The access tier controls, how quickly you'll be able to access blobs in the storage account.
Hot provides quicker access than cool, but at an increased cost.
I should also mention that hot access applies only to blobs and it serves as the default tier for all
new blobs.
Secure transfer required enhances the security of the storage account by only allowing requests to the
storage account through a secure connection.
For example, if making calls with rest APIs to access the storage account, you'd have to use HTTPS.
In other words, requests using HTTP would be rejected if you have secure transfer required enabled.
Virtual networks allow you to limit inbound access to the storage account to only those virtual networks
you specify, and really the number of storage accounts you're going to need in your organization is
typically determined by the data diversity requirements and cost sensitivity in your environment.
For example, organizations will often have offices in different parts of the world, and those offices
will obviously consume data in those locations.
In these cases, organizations often host storage accounts in those geographies where their users are.
So this will affect the number of storage accounts.
Now, while storage accounts themselves cost nothing, the settings that you configure for those storage
accounts will affect the costs of those services that are hosted within the account.
So, for example, geo redundant storage is going to cost more than locally redundant storage and specifying
premium performance and putting data in the hot access tier are both going to increase the cost of blobs.
Deploying multiple storage accounts, though, can help reduce these costs, because what you could
do, for example, is put your critical data in a storage account with geo redundant storage while hosting
your non-critical data in a different storage account with locally redundant storage.
So when you sit the exam, there's a few things you want to remember.
Remember that a storage account is used to group several Azure storage services together in one place
so you can manage them as a group.
Storage accounts include blobs, files, queues and azure tables.
Storage V2 supports all storage types and all of the latest features, while storage or general purpose
V one is a legacy storage account that supports all storage types but may not support all features.
And then blob storage is also a legacy storage account that only supports block blobs and append blobs.
Remember that Microsoft recommends that you use the Storage V two option for all new storage accounts,
and when answering any questions about storage accounts or that involves storage deployment, think
about any diversity requirements or cost requirements that you're presented with in the question.
