48. Configuring Network Access to Azure Storage 
Hello and welcome to configuring network access to storage accounts. To configure the service
endpoints and to control network access for a storage account, you use the firewalls and
virtual networks settings. Basically, what you need to do is specify the virtual networks
that should have access to the storage account. It's not terribly difficult to set up. The
image on your screen shows how this is done. When configuring the firewalls and virtual
network settings, you have a few options as you can see from this screenshot. You can
allow public access to the storage account from all networks, or you can enable it from
specific subnets on virtual networks, public IPs, or even one or more public IP ranges.
You can also disable access altogether. So for example, if you have a storage account
called Surf City Storage and three different virtual networks, what you could do is allow
access to the storage account from some, all, or none of those virtual networks. The diagram
on your screen highlights this type of setup. The caveat here when setting this stuff up
is that any subnets or virtual networks that you want to restrict access to must reside
in the same Azure region or region pair as the storage account you're configuring access
to. The network settings setting allows you to configure how you would want to route traffic
as it travels from its source to the storage account. You can either route the traffic
over the Microsoft network, or you can have it go over the internet. Microsoft routing
is recommended for most customers. Configuring a private endpoint allows you to privately
connect to the storage account via a network interface that uses a private IP address from
your virtual network. And this is done via Azure Private Link. When configuring a private
endpoint, the private endpoint must be in the same region as the virtual network. And
then you have the custom domain option here. Configuring a custom domain allows you to
specify a domain that you want to use to access blob data within the storage account. The image
on your screen highlights how this is done. So when it's time to sit the exam, there's a couple
things you want to remember. First, you want to remember that to configure the service endpoints
and to control network access for a storage account, you use the firewalls and virtual
network settings. This allows you to specify the virtual networks that should have access
to the storage account. Network settings allow you to configure how you would want to route
traffic as it travels from the source to the storage account. And configuring a private
endpoint allows you to privately connect to the storage account via a network interface
that uses a private IP address from your virtual network. This happens over Azure Private Link.
And then a custom domain allows you to specify a domain that you'll use to access blob data
within the Azure storage account.

50. Accessing Azure Storage with Shared Access
Hello and welcome to Shared Access Signatures.
A Shared Access Signature, or SAS,
allows you to provide granular access to files
in Azure Storage. For example, you can use
a Shared Access Signature to grant read-only
or read-write access to files while also
setting an expiration time after which the signature no longer
allows the user to access that data.
In essence, Shared Access Signatures are really
a key that grant permission to a
storage resource. That being the case, a Shared Access
Signature should be protected in much the same way you would
protect an account key. Azure Storage supports three
types of Shared Access Signatures. It supports User Delegation
SAS, Service SAS, and Account SAS.
A User Delegation SAS, or Shared Access
Signature, can only be used for Blob storage. It's secured
with Azure AD credentials. A Service SAS delegates
access to a resource in any one
of the four Azure Storage services, and these are Blob,
Queue, Table, or File. A Service SAS is
secured using a storage account key.
And then you have the Account SAS. This is secured with a storage account
key also. An Account SAS has the same controls
as a Service SAS, but it can also control access to
service-level operations. Now, when you create
a SAS, you specify all the options you need to control,
which include the start time, expiration time, and the permissions
that you want to grant. Using a Shared Access Signature allows
a client to access storage without having direct
access to the storage account credentials. It also allows
you to more granularly control what the
client can access. A Shared Access Signature
actually has two components. Its URI points
to one or more storage resources. The token
specifies how the resources may be accessed by the
client. On your screen, you can see what a typical
SAS URL looks like. The green portion is
the URI, and the blue portion is the token.
The token is going to include several query parameters that will control
how and when the resource is to be accessed.
The signature is then signed with your storage account key when you create
a service or account SAS. If you use an
Azure AD security principle with access to the storage,
you create a User Delegation SAS. Microsoft recommends
that when using a SAS, you always use HTTPS
and that you use a User Delegation
SAS whenever possible, since it really is the most secure option.
That said, User Delegation only works when you're using Azure
AD to manage credentials. That means this option
won't be possible in all situations. The best way to
use a service or account SAS is to associate
the SAS tokens with a stored access policy,
which we'll touch on in the upcoming lesson.

54. Stored Access Policies
Hello and welcome to Stored Access Policies.
As you've already learned, shared access signatures are used to provide client access to Azure Storage
without having to share your Azure credentials.
Now, while this makes it easy to securely grant access to the data, it does create a situation where
anyone with the correct SAS can access your data as long as the SAS is still valid.
This means the only way you can take away access is to regenerate your access keys, which really can
be a pain if you have a bunch of apps that use the existing key.
This is where stored access policies fit in.
More specifically, you can associate your shared access signatures with a stored access policy to help
control who has access to your storage.
Stored access policies can be used with blob containers, file shares, queues and tables.
What a stored access policy does is provide an added level of control over and above the service level
Shared access signatures.
It basically allows you to group shared access signatures and to configure additional restrictions for
signatures that are bound by the policy.
Using a stored access policy allows you to change the start time expiry time and permissions for signature.
It can also be used to revoke the signature after it's been issued.
The image on your screen highlights how easy it is to create a stored access policy for a blob container.
Notice what can be configured here.
You have the identifier, which is essentially the name for the policy.
You have the start time which determines when the policy can start being used and you have the expiry
time which determines when the policy expires.
Now, when a policy does expire, requests to the storage that's controlled by the policy will fail
with a 403 error code.
And then, of course, you have permissions that you want to provide.
You can create stored access policies with C-sharp by using the Azure Portal and through Azure CLI commands.
Now, you're not going to be expected to know how to code policies in C-sharp, so I'm not going to
get into that detail.
But on the off chance you encounter a question about steward access policies and the Azure CLI.
Here's a screengrab of how it would look.
I should point out that Azure allows up to five stored access policies for a single blob container.
So when you set the exam, remember a couple of different things.
Remember that stored access policies can be associated with shared access signatures to help control
who has access to your storage.
Remember that stored access policies can be used with blob containers, file shares, queues and tables
to configure additional restrictions for signatures that are bound by the policy.
And remember that using a stored access policy allows you to change the start time expiry time and permissions
for a signature, and it can be used to revoke the signature after it's been issued.

57. Azure Import / Export Service
The Azure Import Export Service is a service that's used to securely import large amounts of data to
Azure Blob storage and to Azure files.
This is done through the shipment of actual disk drives to an Azure data center.
This service can also be used, however, to transfer data from the Azure blob storage to disk drives
that ship back to your on premises sites.
When leveraging the Azure Import Export Service, what you do is you supply your own disk drives and
you transfer data with the Azure Import Export Service.
You can also use disk drives that are supplied by Microsoft if necessary.
Now, if you want to transfer data using disk drives that are supplied by Microsoft, you need to leverage
the Azure data box disc to import data into Azure.
For this service, Microsoft will ship up to five encrypted, solid state drives with a total capacity
of 40TB per order.
They'll ship these disks directly to your data center through a regional carrier.
What you can then do is configure those disk drives and then copy data to those drives over a USB three
connection.
You'll then ship those drives back to Azure.
Now, as far as the use cases go for Azure import and export, the most common use case is a scenario
where you need to upload or download a lot of data and if you do it over the network, it's going to
be too slow.
For example, you may use the import export service to perform a data migration to Azure.
What this would do is allow you to move large amounts of data to Azure in a quick and cost effective
manner.
You might also use the Azure Import Export service for content distribution.
What you can do then is quickly send your data to customer sites, for example.
Backup is another typical use case for Azure import export.
What you can do is take backups of your on prem data and then store that data in Azure storage.
And on the same token, you can use the Azure import export service for data recovery.
You can use it to recover large amounts of data that's stored in storage and then have it delivered
to your on prem location.
The Import Export Service is comprised of an import export service, a WA import export tool and disk
drives.
The Import Export Service is obviously a service that's available in Azure that allows you through the
portal to create and track data imports and data exports.
The way import export tool is a command line tool that prepares your disk drives that you ship for import.
It facilitates the copying of your data to the drive and encrypts the data on the drive with Bitlocker.
The way import export tool also generates the drive journal files that are used during import creation,
and it helps identify the number of drives that are needed for export jobs.
This tool is available in two different versions Version one and version two.
Microsoft recommends using version one when you're importing or exporting into or from Azure blob storage.
Version two should be used for importing data into Azure files.
It's important to note that the WA import export tool is only compatible with 64 bit Windows operating
systems.
The third component of the import export service are the disk drives.
What you do is you ship solid state drives or hard drives so they can be SSDs or HDDs to the Azure Data
Center.
The Azure Import Export Service allows data transfer into Azure blobs and into Azure files by creating
what are called jobs.
You can use the Azure portal or Azure Resource Manager rest APIs to create jobs.
And each job is associated with a single storage account.
Now, the jobs that you can create can either be import or export jobs.
Obviously, an import job will allow you to import data into Azure blobs or into Azure files.
While the export job allows data to be exported from Azure blobs.
For import jobs.
What you do is ship drives that contain your data.
When you create an export job, what you do is you ship empty drives to the data center.
Now, that said, in each case, you can only ship up to ten disk drives per job.
So that, in a nutshell, gives you some information about how you go about importing and exporting
data into and out of Azure.

58.
Create an Export Job
At a high level, an export job involves the following steps:
1. Determine the data to be exported, number of drives you need, source blobs or container paths of your data in Blob storage.
2. Create an export job in your source storage account in Azure portal.
3. Specify source blobs or container paths for the data to be exported.
4. Provide the return address and carrier account number for shipping the drives back to you.
5. Ship the disk drives to the shipping address provided during job creation.
6. Update the delivery tracking number in the export job details and submit the export job.
7. The drives are received and processed at the Azure data center.
8. The drives are encrypted with BitLocker and the keys are available via the Azure portal.
9. The drives are shipped using your carrier account to the return address provided in the import job.
Note
For local (within data center country/region) shipments, please share a domestic carrier account.
For abroad (outside data center country/region) shipments, please share an international carrier account.
￼

For step-by-step instructions on data export, go to Export data from Azure Blobs.


59.
Create an Import Job
At a high level, an import job involves the following steps:
1. Determine data to be imported, number of drives you need, destination blob location for your data in Azure storage.
2. Use the WAImportExport tool to copy data to disk drives. Encrypt the disk drives with BitLocker.
3. Create an import job in your target storage account in Azure portal. Upload the drive journal files.
4. Provide the return address and carrier account number for shipping the drives back to you.
5. Ship the disk drives to the shipping address provided during job creation.
6. Update the delivery tracking number in the import job details and submit the import job.
7. The drives are received and processed at the Azure data center.
8. The drives are shipped using your carrier account to the return address provided in the import job.
￼
For step-by-step instructions on data import, go to:
* Import data into Azure Blobs
* Import data into Azure Files


60.
Azure Data Box Overview
The Microsoft Azure Data Box offering lets you send terabytes of data into Azure securely, quickly, and inexpensively by shipping you a proprietary Data Box storage device. Each storage device has a maximum usable storage capacity of 80 TB. It's shipped to your datacenter through a regional carrier. The device has a rugged casing to protect and secure data during the transit.
The Data Box device can be ordered via the Azure portal, and once the device is received, you can set it up using the local web UI. You then copy the data from your servers to the device and then you ship the device back to Azure. Microsoft will then upload the data from the device to Azure. The entire process is tracked end-to-end by the Data Box service in the Azure portal.
Typical Use cases
Data Box is perfect in cases where you need to transfer 40TB, or more, of data when there is limited or no network connectivity. There are three ways to leverage Data Box for data transfer to Azure:
* One time migration - large amount of on-premises data is moved to Azure
    * Moving a media library from offline tapes into Azure to create an online media library
    * Migrating a VM farm, SQL server, and applications to Azure
    * Moving historical data to Azure
* Initial bulk transfer - an initial bulk transfer, using Data Box, followed by incremental transfers over the network
    * For example, you can use something like Commvault and Data Box to move initial large historical backup to Azure. Once the data is seeded, incremental data is transferred via network to Azure storage.
* Periodic uploads - when large amount of data is generated periodically and needs to be moved to Azure
Benefits
Data Box is designed to move large amounts of data to Azure with little to no impact to network. The solution has the following benefits:
* Speed - Data Box uses 1 Gbps or 10 Gbps network interfaces to move up to 80 TB of data into Azure
* Secure - Data Box has built-in security protections for the device, data, and the service:
    * Rugged casing secured by tamper-resistant screws and tamper-evident stickers
    * Data on the device is secured with an AES 256-bit encryption at all times
    * Device can only be unlocked with a password provided in the Azure portal
    * The service is protected by the Azure security features
    * Once the data is uploaded to Azure, the disks on the device are wiped clean, in accordance with NIST 800-88r1 standards

62. Azure Files
Hello and welcome to Azure Files.
In this lesson here, we'll take a look at what Azure Files is, where you would use it and what it
offers.
Azure Files is a fully managed file share offering in the cloud.
The file shares offered through Azure files are accessible via SMB and NFS protocols.
That's a server message block and network file system.
You can mount Azure files concurrently from cloud machines and on prem machines.
SMB Azure File shares are accessible from Windows machines, Linux machines and Mac OS machines, while
the NFS Azure file shares are accessible from Linux or Mac OS, you can also cache SMB Azure file shares
on your Windows servers like your on prem Windows servers or even Windows servers in the cloud.
If you wanted to use VMs using what's called Azure file sync with Azure file Sync does is allow for
fast access to your file shares near where the data is actually being used versus where it's stored.
Organisations will often use Azure file shares to replace or supplement their on prem file servers.
They'll also use them to lift and shift applications into the cloud to simplify cloud development,
and they use them to help with the containerization of applications.
Now when I talk about replacing or supplementing on prem file servers, what I'm referring to is, is
the fact that you can use Azure Files to completely replace or like I said, supplement those traditional
on prem file servers or even NAS devices since Windows machines, Mac OS machines and Linux machines
can directly mount those Azure file shares from wherever they are in the world.
The SMB Azure file shares can be replicated using that Azure File Sync service to your Windows servers,
whether they're on prem or in the cloud.
And then what that does is enhance the performance and distributed caching of those file shares.
And then with the release of Azure Files, Active Directory authentication, SMB Azure file shares can
even now work with Active Directory hosted on prem, managing your access control so you can control
authentication for your Azure files with an on prem Active Directory.
Now I also mentioned the lift and shift benefit, and that's because what Azure Files does is simplify
the process for lifting and shifting application lines from an on prem environment to the cloud.
And that's because some applications will typically expect a file share in order to store file application
or user data.
So what Azure Files does is allow both the classic lift and shift scenario where both the app and its
data are moved to the cloud, but it also enables the hybrid lift and shift scenario where the application
gets moved or really the application stays on prem, but the application data gets moved to Azure Files.
And then of course you can have Azure files to simplify your cloud development.
And there's a couple of different ways it does this.
For example, applications will often need a place to store their settings.
So in the case of shared application settings, those distributed applications can store their configuration
files in a centralized location, that centralized location being Azure files, and then the applications
can load them through the Azure File Rest API.
And Azure file share is also a good place for cloud applications to write their logs or their metrics
or crash dump, stuff like that.
In those cases, the logs are written by the application instances through the rest API, and then your
developers can access them by mounting the file share on their local machine.
And of course you have dev test and debug and situations where you have admins or developers working
on virtual machines In Azure, they'll often need different tools or utilities at their disposal.
So what you can do is either copy these tools and utilities to each VM so they have access to them from
each VM.
Or you can simply mount an Azure file, share locally on the VMs and then copy all the tools there so
your developers and admins can get quicker access to those tools and utilities without having to copy
them in a one off fashion.
And I did mention containerization earlier, and what I meant by containerization is that Azure File
shares can actually be used as persistent volumes for stateful containers.
So what that means is that any containers that have to access raw data at every startup, the shared
file system is required to allow those containers to access that file system no matter which instance
they run on.
And that's where these Azure file shares really shine as persistent volumes.
So what you really want to remember about Azure Files is that they're a fully managed file share system
in the cloud and they're accessible via both SMB and NFS.
Just keep that in mind.
That's likely to come up.
The Azure file shares can be mounted concurrently by both on prem machines and cloud machines, and
they're accessible from, at least in the case of SMB, Windows, Linux and Mac OS clients.
While the NFS Azure file shares are accessible from Linux or Mac OS.
So keep that in mind as well.
You're likely to see that something being showing up on those exams.
And you should also remember that Azure Files are useful for replacing or supplementing on prem file
servers.
Lift and shift.
They simplify cloud development and they can be helpful with containerisation of apps.

64. Azure Blob Storage 
Hello and welcome to Azure Blobs.
Over the next few minutes, we'll take a look at what Azure blob storage is and what it's designed for.
Azure Blob Storage is really Microsoft's object storage solution for the cloud.
It's optimized so that you can use it to store massive amounts of unstructured data.
Now, unstructured data is data that doesn't adhere to a particular data model or definition or schema.
Think text or binary data.
More specifically, when Microsoft designed blob storage, it was designed for serving images or documents
in a browser for storing files that are going to have distributed access.
It's really good for streaming video and audio or writing to log files.
And organizations can also use blob storage for storing data for backup and restore disaster recovery.
ARCHIVAL And they can also use it for storing data, for analysis, whether they're going to use some
on prem solution to analyze that data or an Azure hosted service objects that are stored in blob storage
can be accessed via HTTP or via HTTPS.
You can also access them via the Azure Storage Rest API, Azure PowerShell Azure CLI or through an Azure
storage client library.
I should also mention that Blob Storage supports Azure Data Lake Storage Gen two.
Now, what this is, is Microsoft's Enterprise big data analytics solution for the Cloud Azure Data
Lake Storage Gen two offers a hierarchical file system along with the advantages of blob storage, including,
for example, low cost storage that's tiered high availability, consistency and disaster recovery capabilities.
If you want to learn more about the details of data Lake Storage Gen two, you can visit the URL on
your screen.
When discussing blob storage, you need to be familiar with three types of resources the storage account,
a container in the storage account, which is a blob container, and the blob itself which resides in
the container.
The diagram on your screen shows the relationship between these resources.
You'll see here that the container sits within the storage account and then the blobs are housed within
the container.
It's also important to remember that storage is often accessed at different rates depending on the use.
Now, because of this, Azure storage offers different access tiers for your blob storage.
You have the hot access tier, which is optimised for storing data that's accessed frequently.
Think of images for your website.
The hot access tier has the highest storage costs, but the lowest access costs.
You then have the cool access tier, which is optimised for data that's infrequently accessed but stored
for at least 30 days.
An example of this type of use case would be maybe invoices for customers.
The cool access tier has lower storage costs than the hot tier, but also higher access costs than the
hot access tier.
And then the last tier is the archive access tier.
Now the archive access tier is really designed for data that's going to be rarely accessed and it's
going to be stored for at least 180 days.
And the third piece is that you have to have flexible latency requirements.
So for example, the archive access tier would be good for long term backups.
Now this tier is going to offer the lowest storage costs, but also the highest access costs.
So all that said, when you sit the exam, what you want to do is remember that blob storage is optimized
for storing massive amounts of unstructured data.
Keep the unstructured data piece in mind.
You also need to remember the use cases for blob storage, and you should also remember and be able
to describe the different blob storage tiers.

66. Azure Storage Replication - LRS vs GRS
Hello and welcome to LRS versus GRS.
In this lesson, we'll take a look at the differences between locally redundant storage and zone redundant
storage.
Now, as your storage is always going to store multiple copies of your data so it's protected from both
planned and unplanned events.
Now these types of events could include things like transient hardware failures, network outages,
power outages and even natural disasters.
This redundancy is what's going to ensure that your storage account meets availability and durability
targets during these failures.
So when you're deciding which redundancy option to choose for your particular business scenario, what
you need to do is consider the trade offs between lower costs and higher availability.
So, for example, you're going to want to think about how your data gets replicated within the primary
region, whether your data is replicated to a secondary region that's geographically distant to your
primary region, so you can protect against things like regional disasters.
This, by the way, would be geo replication.
And then you also need to think about whether your application is going to need read access to that
replicated data in your secondary region in the event that the primary region goes down.
So this type of replication would be called geo replication with read access.
And we'll get into some of these different types of replication and storage options later on.
Now, I should mention that the availability and features that we're talking about here would also be
available to accounts that have hierarchical namespaces.
In other words, blob storage.
So before we get into the actual redundancy options of LSE and XSS, I just want to make sure that we
touch on the idea that services that comprise Azure Storage are managed through the storage account.
So we're just going to touch on the different pieces of storage here.
That storage account is going to represent a shared pool of storage that you can use to deploy storage
resources like blob containers, as in blob storage file shares in Azure files, tables and table storage
and queues and queue storage when you set a redundancy option for a storage account.
That redundancy setting is going to be shared for all of the storage services within that account.
So in other words, everything deployed within that storage account will have the same redundancy setting.
If you need to separate things out.
In other words, isolate the different types of resources with different redundancy requirements, then
what you would do is you would deploy them into separate storage accounts.
So let's talk a little bit about the redundancy in the primary region.
So when you store data in an Azure storage account, that data is always going to be replicated three
times within the primary region.
That being the case, there's two different options for you to choose from regarding how that data gets
replicated.
You have locally redundant storage or less, which copies your data synchronously three times within
a single physical location within the primary region.
And then you have zone redundant storage which copies your data synchronously across three Azure availability
zones within the primary region.
Now, what locally redundant storage does or loads is it replicates your storage account three times
within a single data center within your primary region.
As a result of this, LIRs can offer 11 nines of durability over the course of a given year.
I should mention that LIRs is the lowest cost redundancy option available.
However, with that, affordability comes a trade off.
And what that means is RS also offers the least durability compared to other options.
What LIRs is designed to do is protect data against server, rack and drive failures.
And that's it.
So what that means is if that you have a disaster like a fire or flood within the data center, all
replicas of that storage account using ours might be lost or unrecoverable.
Now, I should also mention that a write request to a storage account that's using us is going to happen
synchronously.
And that right option only returns successfully after that data gets written to all three replicas.
That's how we ensure that we have that that replication or that redundancy, I should say.
Generally speaking, what you'd use us for is a scenario where you have an application that stores data
that can be easily reconstructed if data loss occurs.
You might also use less if you have an application that you have to restrict to replicating data only
within a specific country or region due to governance requirements.
And you would also use LIRs if you're using Azure unmanaged disks, which is becoming less and less
frequent.
But if that's the case, you'd use less now zone redundant storage or ZTS replicates your storage account
synchronously across three Azure availability zones in the primary region.
Now each availability zone is a separate physical location, and each of these separate physical locations
has their own independent power, their own cooling, their own networking.
And because of that, Zeiss offers durability for storage in those storage accounts of 12/9 over a given
year versus the 11 nines that ours offers.
So it's significant when you use ZFS, your data is still accessible for read and write operations,
even if a zone becomes unavailable.
And when a write request is made to a storage account that's using XSS, that right request happens
synchronously and that write operation is only going to return successful once the data has been written
to all replicas across all the three availability zones.
I should point out that Microsoft recommends using Z ers in the primary region in situations where you're
going to require high availability.
Microsoft also recommends GRS if you need to restrict replication of your data to within a country or
region for data governance requirements.
Microsoft also recommends ERS for Azure Files workloads.
If you take a look on your screen, you'll see what Xr's looks like.
It's also good to remember that GRS is supported for all Azure storage services through what's called
a standard general purpose V two storage account.
So in other words, when using the general purpose V two storage account, XR is supported by Azure
Blob Storage.
Azure Files, Azure Table Storage and Azure Q Storage.
So some things to remember when sitting the exam are the fact that the redundancy options that we just
talked about apply to storage in the primary region.
What you're going to want to do is remember what this is or locally redundant storage is what ZFS is
or zone redundant storage and what the availability is for each.
You're also going to want to remember which types of Azure storage support each type of redundancy option.
And you'll also want to remember the typical use cases for each type of redundancy.
And you'd also do yourself a big favor if you can remember that this is the cheapest redundancy option.

Hello and welcome to GRS versus Geysers.
In this lesson, we'll take a look at geo redundant storage or GRS and geo Zone redundant storage,

Now for applications that are going to require high durability, what you can do is choose to copy the

We're talking hundreds of miles by copying your data in that storage account to a secondary region.

Geo redundant storage or GRS is used to copy that data synchronously three times within a physical location

But it then copies that data asynchronously to a nother single physical location in a secondary region.

Copies your data synchronously across three Azure availability zones in the primary region using zone

But then it also copies your data asynchronously to another single physical location in the secondary

S.

As I mentioned, the data within the secondary region is always replicated synchronously three times

there.

Lots of acronyms to remember here, but make sure you remember them when you enable that.
Read access to the secondary region that allows your data to become always available to be read from

Read Access Jio Redundant storage ragers or read access Jio Redundant storage or ragers configurations

Now, geo Zone redundant storage combines the high availability that's provided by redundancy across
availability zones with protection from regional outages, which is provided by JIO replication.
Microsoft actually recommends using GRS for any applications that are going to need maximum consistency,
durability, high availability, high end performance or resilience.
For DRT.
When you're using a GCR storage account, you can continue to read and write data if an availability
zone becomes unavailable.
And additionally your data is also durable in the case of a complete regional outage or disaster in
which the primary region can be recovered.
I should mention that geysers provides at least 16 nines of durability over a given year.
The diagram on your screen shows how your data is replicated using geysers or ray geysers.
Now, I do want to point out that only standard general purpose v two storage accounts support geysers.
That said, geysers is supported by all of the storage services, including Azure Blob Storage, Azure
files as your table storage and Azure queue storage.
So when it's time to sit the exam, what you want to do is remember a few different things.

region.
Be sure to remember what GRS is and what geysers is.
You'll also want to keep in mind what the availability is for each and which types of Azure storage
services support each type of redundancy.
You should also remember what the typical use cases are for each type of redundancy that we've covered.
And you should also be able to explain what our G.

of your VM deployment.

So for this demonstration, we're going to deploy into East US.
Now in this availability options dropdown, we have a couple of different options we can deploy into
an availability zone or an availability set.
Now, if we hover over the icon here and click Learn more, we can see that Microsoft has some really
good information on availability zones versus availability sets.
Now the long and short of it is that in a VM availability set, your virtual machines are protected
from hardware failures within a data center.
So if you lose a rack within a data center, an availability set will make sure that your VMs.
Of course you'll have to have more than one in your availability set, but it'll make sure that both
of your VMs will remain separate on different hardware.
So if a particular rack goes down within a data center, at least one of your VMs remains running.
Now an availability zone is a little bit different.
While an availability set protects against a hardware failure within a data center, when an availability
zone does, is protect your VMs from the loss of an entire data center.
Essentially, your VMs are replicated to multiple zones within different data centers.
So what that means if a particular data center in a specific region goes down, you're still protected
because your VMs are separated completely from each data center.
Now what I would do here is make sure you read over this page on availability options.
It will give you lots of good information on availability zones.
What they offer more specifically, talks a little bit about the fault domains and the update domains
which are essentially logical groupings of underlying hardware that further protect your VMs from failure.
Now, that being said, let's close this out and we'll go back over here.
And what we're going to do for this demonstration is we're going to deploy our virtual machine in an
availability set.
Now, when I do that, I get another dropdown here and Azure asks me, you know, where do you want
to put it?
Which availability set?
This dropdown will give us any existing availability sets that we currently have deployed and in our
case we have none.
So we really have only one option here and that's to create a new availability set.
Now it's important to note that when you deploy into an availability set.
If you don't deploy your VM into an availability set at the time of creation, you can't add it to an
availability set later on.
So let's go ahead and create a new availability set.
And this is essentially a container.
There's not a whole lot of information here to configure.
So what we'll do here is we'll call our availability set.
Avail set zero one.
Now, when we deploy this availability set, we need to configure the fault domains and update domains
within it.
If we hover over the icon for fault domains, we can see here that virtual machines that are in the
same fault domain will share common power and a common physical network switch.
Hovering over the icon for update domains tells us that VMs in the same update domain will be restarted
together during planned maintenance.
It also tells us here that Azure won't ever restart more than one update domain at a time.
So what does that mean?
Well, from a fault domain perspective, let's say we deploy two VMs into this availability set.
And we have two fault domains defined.
What Azure will do is ensure that both of those VMs are in different fault domains.
So we'll have server one in fault domain one and then server two in fault domain two.
That way, if anything happens within that fault domain, that that common power source, only one of
the VMs within our availability set is affected.
So our application continues to run, assuming it's built with high availability within the app itself.
So the idea of a fault domain is to make sure that if there is a fault, hence the name fault domain,
that only a subset of our VMs in the availability set are affected.
Update domains is is similar, but it revolves around updates.
So again, let's take the example of Microsoft and server one and server two.
So we have server one goes into update domain one, server two goes into update, domain two.
If Microsoft has to perform maintenance in the background and needs to restart virtual machines, it's
only going to do one update domain at a time.
So in our case, we have five different update domains by default.
So in theory, if we deployed five VMs, each one would go into a separate update domain, which means
Microsoft would never take down more than one of our VMs at the same time because they're updating each
domain one at a time.
Now, if we had six VMs, what would happen is in this case with five update domains defined, the first
five VMs would go through update, update domains one through five, and then the sixth server we deploy
would go back into update domain one, and then in the seventh server would go into two.
ET cetera.
ET cetera.
So in this case, we only have two VMs affected potentially if an update were to occur because Microsoft
would only restart the VMs in one update domain at a time.
Now the defaults here are two fault domains and five update domains, so we'll leave these alone here.
That works for me.

So we'll leave this grayed out.
And as a matter of fact, we couldn't even use a host group because dedicated hosts are not supported
with availability sets, which again, we're deploying this VM into an availability set.
The Proximity Placement group allows you to group Azure resources physically together or closer together,
I should say, within the same region.
For example, let's say you have a Great Plains application server and that Great Plains application
server is back ended by a SQL Server.
So server one is a great plain server and server two is a SQL box that Great Plains is talking to.
What you would typically want to do is ensure that any VMs that need to talk to one another that are
that should be physically close together can be by using a proximity placement group.
If we click Learn more here, we can see that proximity placement groups allow you to get your VMs as
close as possible, which in turn reduces the latency.
So again, you have an application server talking directly to a SQL box.
You want that latency to be as low as possible.
You could do that with a proximity placement group.
We'll close this out.
Now, we don't have any proximity placement groups found, so that's why we don't have any in our dropdown
here.
And then lastly, we have VM generation.
And as you can see here, Gen two VMs will support things like Uefi based boot architecture, higher
memory amounts, larger OS disk sizes, some of the more specific hardware options that are available.
Now that being the case, Gen two VMs do not yet support some Azure platform features like oddly enough,
Azure disk encryption.
So you could actually deploy a Gen two VM to get some cool access to other features, but you'd lose
Azure disk encryption.
So for this demonstration here, we'll go with Gen one.

71.
HANDS-ON AZURE LAB SIMULATION: Create and Attach a VM Data Disk
Lab Overview
Azure managed disks are block-level storage volumes that are managed by Azure and used with Azure Virtual Machines. Managed disks are like a physical disk in an on-premises server but, virtualized. With managed disks, all you have to do is specify the disk size, the disk type, and provision the disk. Once you provision the disk, Azure handles the rest.
The available types of disks are ultra disks, premium solid-state drives (SSD), standard SSDs, and standard hard disk drives (HDD).
Learning Objectives
In this Azure simulation, you will create a new data disk and attach it to an existing virtual machine.
Since this lab involves interactive tasks in a simulated Azure portal, it's best to perform the lab from a desktop or laptop.

75. DEMO: Redeploying VMs
Hello and welcome back.
What we're going to do here in this demonstration is Redeploy virtual machine.
Now, generally speaking, you would redeploy a VM if you run into a situation where you've maybe restarted
it and all of a sudden you can't get to it via RDP anymore.
By redeploying it, you kind of give it a fresh start.
It doesn't affect any applications or anything running on the VM.
All it does is really take that VM, bring it down and start it on a new host in the underlying data
center within Azure.
Now to redeploy a VM, what we do from the overview page here for that VM and you can see here we're
at that overview page.
If we scroll down here toward the bottom here, you'll see under support and troubleshooting the option
to redeploy that virtual machine.
Now if we select redeploy here, basically Microsoft tells us, it says, hey, you know what?
If you can't connect to your VM, try to redeploy it.
And what this redeployment does is it migrates it to a new host.
Now, the one caveat here is that when you do redeploy a virtual machine, that virtual machine is obviously
restarted because it's moving to another host.
But you're also going to lose any data on the temporary drive.
That's usually not an issue, but it's something to be aware of.
You'll also notice that the virtual machine will be unavailable while that redeployment process occurs.
Now, to initialize the redeployment, we simply click the redeployment button down here at the bottom.
And then what Azure is going to do is restart that VM, but on different hardware within Azure.
And we can see with the notification bell that the task is still running and this takes a few minutes.
Remember, you're you're bringing a machine down, moving it and then bringing it back up.
So it takes a few minutes for the redeployment to complete.
If we click on the bell, we can see that the task is, in fact, still running.
We'll close that out.
And we can see it's successfully been redeployed.
Now, if we click on Learn more here for Redeploy, I want to do this because I want to make sure I
send you over to Microsoft's documentation to kind of get some underlying context.
And again, the purpose of this course is really to get your hands dirty on actually doing things.
So you learn how to do them in a real world environment.
I'm not doing a whole lot with slides and lectures here because the course is long enough just with
all the hands on stuff.
I want to make sure that you get that context by reading some of the official Microsoft documentation.
Not to mention if I create a bunch of slides and lectures, they're probably going to be outdated by
next week because Microsoft seems to change things rather frequently.
So I'd rather direct you to this documentation than create stuff that could be out of date pretty quickly.
And so be sure to take this quick two minute read on redeploying Windows virtual machines so you get
an understanding of some of the underlying theory that we just talked about when we redeployed our VM.
So I'll go ahead and close this tab out.
And if we bounce back over to overview, we can see our VM is in the running state.

Lab Overview
If you have been facing difficulties troubleshooting Remote Desktop (RDP) connection or application access to Windows-based Azure virtual machine (VM), redeploying the VM may help. When you redeploy a VM, Azure will shut down the VM, move the VM to a new node within the Azure infrastructure, and then power it back on, retaining all your configuration options and associated resources.
If a virtual machine is in a failed state, you can reapply the virtual machine’s state. This operation will rerun VM provisioning and help solve the VM failed state.

80. DEMO: Deploying and Configuring Scale Sets
Hello and welcome to Deploying and Configuring scale sets.
Now a virtual machine scale set is used so that you can deploy and manage sets.
Hence the name scale set sets of Autoscaling virtual machines.
These Autoscaling VMs can scale up or they can scale down based on resource utilization like CPU or
memory demand or even network traffic.
You can also scale them up and down manually.
Essentially what happens is you're creating a pool of VMs that are identically configured and they sit
behind an Azure load balancer, which then distributes incoming traffic to those VMs in the scale set.
Now this demonstration is going to show you how to create a scale set.
Now we're going to cover a little bit of load balancer here.
So we're jumping a little bit out of order within the course itself.
We're not going to get into the actual details of creating the load balancer because I want to explain
how that's done later on.
But for this instance here, we'll zip through the creation of the load balancer just so we have that
in place, because that is necessary to use a scale set.
So on the screen here, I'm logged into my Azure portal.
What I'm going to do from my home screen is I'll bounce over into Azure Labs.
Now from Azure Labs here, what I'm going to do is create a load balancer.
It's going to be a standard public load balancer so that can front end my scale set.

scale this scale set or perform some kind of custom policy.
We can see here that the manual option will cause the scale set to maintain a fixed instance count that
we control manually.
While the custom option allows auto scale to scale the capacity of the VM scale set on a schedule or
based on metrics.
So if we select custom here now, if we hover over the minimum number of VMs, we can see that's exactly
what this is.
It's the minimum value for auto scale on the scale set.
The ten here for maximum is the maximum number of VMs within the scale set.
Now in the scale out section here, we can tell the scale set at what CPU threshold the scale set should
scale out or add a VM.
In this case, the default setting is 75%.
So when the CPU across all existing instances within the scale set hits 75% on average.
Another instance will be added to the scale set.
Now this duration and minutes specifies how long that threshold needs to be met.
So what this is telling us here that if our scale set hits 75% utilization on the CPU for a duration
of ten minutes, we want to scale it out.
And then the number of VMs to increase by is obviously the number of VMs to add to the scale set as
long as this scale out threshold is met.
Scale in simply tells Azure, okay, if we drop back down to a specific CPU threshold, then what we
can do is we can decrease our scale set by the number of VMs we specify here.
Now we have some diagnostics information we can enable and then we can look at the scale in policy and
we have a couple of different options for scale in default.
Newest VM or oldest VM.
The default scale in policy means that the scaling operation will balance everything across availability
zones and fault domains and then delete the VM with the highest instance ID in a case when scaling occurs.
The newest VM policy deletes the newest created VM, while the oldest VM policy deletes the oldest created
VM.
We'll leave this at the default.

HANDS-ON AZURE LAB SIMULATION: Deploy and Configure a VM Scaleset in Azure
Lab Overview
In this lab, you’ll learn how to deploy and configure an Azure Virtual Machine Scale Set (VMSS) using the Azure portal.
A VM Scale Set is an Azure compute resource that allows you to deploy and manage a set of identical VMs. This lab walks you through the process of setting up the scale set, configuring auto-scaling based on CPU usage, and attaching storage to the VMs.
You’ll also configure network settings and management options to ensure smooth operations.
By the end of this lab, you’ll have deployed a scalable, flexible infrastructure capable of automatically scaling in or out based on workload demands.
Learning Objectives
* Understand how to create and configure an Azure Virtual Machine Scale Set.
* Learn how to configure availability zones for high availability.
* Gain hands-on experience configuring auto-scaling rules based on CPU utilization.
* Set up and manage storage options for the virtual machines in the scale set.
* Configure networking and security settings for the VM Scale Set.
* Review and deploy a fully configured VM Scale Set, and learn how to monitor and manage it post-deployment.
To Access the Lab:
Ensure that you're logged into labITpro with the free "Azure Administrator Lab Series" subscription you setup in "Course Setup" earlier in this course, and then click here to access the lab.

86. 
A Word About Custom Script Extensions
Custom Script Extensions are used to configure VMs and to deploy software post-deployment.
The Custom Script Extension downloads and executes scripts on Azure virtual machines. Scripts used with the extension can be downloaded from Azure storage or even from GitHub. They can also be provided to the Azure portal at extension run time. The Custom Script extension integrates with Azure Resource Manager templates, and can also be run using the Azure CLI, PowerShell, Azure portal, or the Azure Virtual Machine REST API.
You can use the custom script extension to use your Azure Blob storage credentials, to access Azure Blob storage. Alternatively, the script location can be anywhere, as long as the VM can route to that end point, such as GitHub, internal file servers, etc.

HANDS-ON AZURE LAB SIMULATION: Create and Configure an Azure Virtual Network
Lab Overview
In this lab, you’ll learn how to create and configure a Virtual Network (vNet) and Subnet in Microsoft Azure.
Virtual networks are essential for organizing and managing network traffic within the Azure environment, and subnets allow you to segment your vNet for better control over resource communication.
This lab walks you through the process of creating a new vNet, configuring address spaces, and setting up subnets to meet the needs of different applications. You’ll also apply network security features like encryption and use tags to categorize your vNet.
Learning Objectives
* Understand how to create a Virtual Network in Azure.
* Learn how to configure address spaces and assign custom IP ranges for your vNet.
* Gain experience setting up subnets to segment resources within a vNet.
* Apply encryption to secure network traffic within your virtual network.
* Use tags to categorize and manage virtual networks for production or development environments.
* Review and finalize the vNet and subnet settings before deployment.

Now all this is going to do is create the network security group resource.

So let's go ahead and pin it to our dashboard here and open it up.
When we open the overview page of our new network security group, we can see.
That the network security group is not associated with any subnets nor any network interfaces.
And it has no custom security rules defined.
What you do see, however, is that the network security group, when it's deployed, is deployed with
a set of default inbound rules and a set of default outbound rules.
The allow Vnet inbound inbound security rule is configured by default to allow any any between the virtual
network and itself.
This allows any VMs that are deployed to the virtual network to communicate with one another.
The allow Azure Load Balancer Inbound rule is configured to allow any protocol in from an Azure load
balancer.
So if we deploy an Azure load balancer, this rule will allow traffic to come from it.
The third default inbound security rule is called deny all inbound.
And it does exactly what it says.
It denies all traffic from all sources.
So unless traffic is coming in from the virtual network or from a load balancer, this rule blocks it.
So this essentially isolates the network or Nic, that this network security group applies to unless
the traffic is coming from a virtual network or from a load balancer.
The outbound security rules are essentially the same thing, but in reverse.
The allow Vnet outbound rule allows all traffic from the virtual network to the virtual network.
It allows all outbound internet traffic from anywhere on the virtual network or nick that it's applied
to via the allow internet outbound rule and the deny all outbound again does what it says.
It blocks everything outbound from everywhere.
To associate this network security group with our newly created subnet.
What we need to do here is click on subnets.
And from here we can click the associate button and then select the virtual network.
We want to apply this network security group to.
We're going to do here is apply it to the or associate it with, I should say, to use the right terminology.
We're going to associate this network security group with the Vnet as one network and more specifically
the subnet as one.
Keep in mind that a network security group is associated with a subnet.
It's not associated with a Vnet.
If we click okay here, we can see that the network security group protects the subnet.
There's nowhere in here where you're going to define what virtual network it protects.
It actually protects the subnet itself or NICs, and we'll do that later on.
So at this point, we have a default configuration network security group deployed and associated with
our subnet as one virtual subnet, which is part of our virtual network.

Lab Overview
In this lab, you will learn how to create and configure a Network Security Group (NSG) within the Azure portal. Network Security Groups are critical components in Azure that help you control inbound and outbound network traffic to your resources.
By setting up rules in an NSG, you can define which traffic is allowed or denied based on factors such as the source, destination, and protocol.
This hands-on lab will guide you step-by-step through the process of creating a new NSG, defining security rules, and associating the NSG with a subnet in a virtual network.
By the end of this lab, you will have a fully functional NSG that controls traffic for a specified subnet in your virtual network.

* Create a new Network Security Group.
* Define inbound security rules to control traffic to your resources.
* Apply specific security rules for HTTP traffic using an NSG.
* Associate the NSG with a subnet in a virtual network.
* Verify the successful application of the NSG to a virtual network and its subnets.
* Understand how to configure and apply network security at the subnet level in Azure.

97.
About vNet Peering
vNet peering allows you to seamlessly connect Azure virtual networks. Once they've been peered, the vNets will appear as one network, for connectivity purposes. Traffic between virtual machines in the peered vNets is routed through the Microsoft backbone infrastructure, through private IP addresses only.
Azure supports the following peering options:
* VNet peering - connecting VNets within the same Azure region
* Global VNet peering - connecting VNets across Azure regions
Benefits of using virtual network peering:
* Network traffic between peered vNets is private, and kept on the Microsoft backbone network. vNet peering requires no public Internet, gateways, or encryption.
* vNet peering offers a low-latency, high-bandwidth connection between resources in different virtual networks.
* Peered vNets offer the ability for resources in one vNet to communicate with resources in a different vNet.
* Peering vNets allows the transfer of data across Azure subscriptions, deployment models, and regions.
* Configuring peering requires no downtime to resources in either virtual network when creating the peering, or after the peering is created.
Network latency between VMs in peered virtual networks within the same region is the same as the latency within a single vNet. Network throughput is based largely on the bandwidth that's allowed for the virtual machine. There are no additional restrictions on bandwidth within the peering.
vNet peering does not require the use of gateways, gateway subnets, or VPN connections.
A network security group that's applied in either virtual network can block access to the other connected virtual networks, if desired. During peering configuration, you can open or close NSG rules between the peered virtual networks. Open full connectivity between peered virtual networks is the default option, but you can apply network security groups to specific subnets or virtual machines to block or deny specific access.

99.
HANDS-ON AZURE LAB SIMULATION: Create and Configure vNet Peering in Azure
Lab Overview
In this lab, you’ll learn how to create and configure Virtual Network (vNet) Peering in Azure. vNet Peering allows you to connect two virtual networks, enabling low-latency, high-speed communication between them as if they were part of a single network.
You’ll walk through the process of setting up bi-directional peering between two virtual networks, ensuring secure communication and resource access across the networks.
By the end of the lab, you’ll have a fully functional vNet Peering setup, allowing for efficient network connectivity between isolated virtual networks.
Learning Objectives
* Understand how to navigate the Azure portal to manage virtual networks.
* Learn how to create vNet Peering between two Azure virtual networks.
* Gain hands-on experience configuring and naming peering links to ensure proper management and tracking.
* Understand the importance of bi-directional peering for secure communication between networks.
* Verify the successful creation and functionality of vNet Peering in the Azure portal.
To Access the Lab:
Ensure that you're logged into labITpro with the free "Azure Administrator Lab Series" subscription you setup in "Course Setup" earlier in this course, and then click here to access the lab.


100. The components of a Site-to-Site VPN
The Components of a Site-to-Site VPN
The architecture of a typical site-to-site VPN consists of the following components:
On-premises network
A private local-area network running within an organization.
VPN appliance
An on-prem VPN device or service that provides external connectivity. to the on-prem network. The on-prem VPN device can be a hardware device or it can be a software solution.
Virtual network (VNet)
The cloud application and the components for the Azure VPN gateway reside in the same VNet.
Azure VPN gateway
The VPN gateway serves as a VPN endpoint in Azure. It allows you to connect the VNet to an on-premises network through a VPN appliance. A VPN gateway consists of the following components: a virtual network gateway, a local network gateway, a connection, and a gateway subnet.
The Virtual network gateway acts as a virtual VPN appliance for the VNet. It's responsible for routing traffic from the on-prem network to the VNet. The Local network gateway represents the on-premises VPN appliance. Network traffic from Azure to the on-prem network is routed through this gateway.
The connection defines the connection type (IPSec) and the shared key that establish connectivity with the on-premises VPN appliance. These components also encrypt traffic. The Gateway subnet hosts the virtual network gateway. This subnet must never host any user-created resources.

109. Availability Sets vs. Availability Zones
Availability Sets vs Availability Zones
Availability Sets
An availability set is a logical grouping of VMs within a datacenter that allows Azure to understand how your application is built to provide for redundancy and availability. We recommended that two or more VMs are created within an availability set to provide for a highly available application and to meet the 99.95% Azure SLA. There is no cost for the Availability Set itself, you only pay for each VM instance that you create. When a single VM is using Azure premium SSDs, the Azure SLA applies for unplanned maintenance events.
An availability set is composed of two additional groupings that protect against hardware failures and allow updates to safely be applied - fault domains (FDs) and update domains (UDs). You can read more about how to manage the availability of Linux VMs or Windows VMs.
Fault domains
A fault domain is a logical group of underlying hardware that share a common power source and network switch, similar to a rack within an on-premises datacenter. As you create VMs within an availability set, the Azure platform automatically distributes your VMs across these fault domains. This approach limits the impact of potential physical hardware failures, network outages, or power interruptions.
Update domains
An update domain is a logical group of underlying hardware that can undergo maintenance or be rebooted at the same time. As you create VMs within an availability set, the Azure platform automatically distributes your VMs across these update domains. This approach ensures that at least one instance of your application always remains running as the Azure platform undergoes periodic maintenance. The order of update domains being rebooted may not proceed sequentially during planned maintenance, but only one update domain is rebooted at a time.
Managed Disk fault domains
For VMs using Azure Managed Disks, VMs are aligned with managed disk fault domains when using a managed availability set. This alignment ensures that all the managed disks attached to a VM are within the same managed disk fault domain. Only VMs with managed disks can be created in a managed availability set. The number of managed disk fault domains varies by region - either two or three managed disk fault domains per region. You can read more about these managed disk fault domains for Linux VMs or Windows VMs.

Availability Zones
Availability zones, an alternative to availability sets, expand the level of control you have to maintain the availability of the applications and data on your VMs. An Availability Zone is a physically separate zone within an Azure region. There are three Availability Zones per supported Azure region. Each Availability Zone has a distinct power source, network, and cooling. By architecting your solutions to use replicated VMs in zones, you can protect your apps and data from the loss of a datacenter. If one zone is compromised, then replicated apps and data are instantly available in another zone.

If a hardware or software failure occurs, only a subset of the VMs is impacted, and the overall solution
that's based on those VMs should continue to operate.

112. About Custom Script Extensions
About Custom Script Extensions
The Custom Script Extension downloads and executes scripts on Azure virtual machines. This extension is useful for post deployment configuration, software installation, or any other configuration / management task. Scripts used with the extension can be downloaded from Azure storage or even from GitHub. They can also be provided to the Azure portal at extension run time. The Custom Script extension integrates with Azure Resource Manager templates, and can also be run using the Azure CLI, PowerShell, Azure portal, or the Azure Virtual Machine REST API.
You can use the custom script extension to use your Azure Blob storage credentials, to access Azure Blob storage. Alternatively, the script location can be anywhere, as long as the VM can route to that end point, such as GitHub, internal file servers, etc.

114. About Azure Load Balancers
About Azure Load Balancers
With Azure Load Balancer, you can scale your applications and create high availability for your services. Load Balancer supports inbound and outbound scenarios, provides low latency and high throughput, and scales up to millions of flows for all TCP and UDP applications.
Load Balancer distributes new inbound flows that arrive on the Load Balancer's frontend to backend pool instances, according to rules and health probes.
Additionally, a public Load Balancer can provide outbound connections for virtual machines (VMs) inside your virtual network by translating their private IP addresses to public IP addresses.
Azure Load Balancer is available in two SKUs: Basic and Standard. There are differences in scale, features, and pricing. Any scenario that's possible with Basic Load Balancer can also be created with Standard Load Balancer, although the approaches might differ slightly. As you learn about Load Balancer, it is important to familiarize yourself with the fundamentals and SKU-specific differences.
Why use Load Balancer?
You can use Azure Load Balancer to:
* Load-balance incoming internet traffic to your VMs. This configuration is known as a Public Load Balancer.
* Load-balance traffic across VMs inside a virtual network. You can also reach a Load Balancer front end from an on-premises network in a hybrid scenario. Both scenarios use a configuration that is known as an Internal Load Balancer.
* Port forward traffic to a specific port on specific VMs with inbound network address translation (NAT) rules.
* Provide outbound connectivity for VMs inside your virtual network by using a public Load Balancer.
Public Load Balancer
A public Load Balancer maps the public IP address and port number of incoming traffic to the private IP address and port number of the VM, and vice versa for the response traffic from the VM. By applying load-balancing rules, you can distribute specific types of traffic across multiple VMs or services. For example, you can spread the load of web request traffic across multiple web servers.
The following figure shows a load-balanced endpoint for web traffic that is shared among three VMs for the public and TCP port 80. These three VMs are in a load-balanced set.
￼
When internet clients send webpage requests to the public IP address of a web app on TCP port 80, Azure Load Balancer distributes the requests across the three VMs in the load-balanced set. For more information about Load Balancer algorithms, see the Load Balancer features section of this article.
By default, Azure Load Balancer distributes network traffic equally among multiple VM instances. You can also configure session affinity. For more information, see Load Balancer distribution mode.
Internal Load Balancer
An internal Load Balancer directs traffic only to resources that are inside a virtual network or that use a VPN to access Azure infrastructure. In this respect, an internal Load Balancer differs from a public Load Balancer. Azure infrastructure restricts access to the load-balanced frontend IP addresses of a virtual network. Frontend IP addresses and virtual networks are never directly exposed to an internet endpoint. Internal line-of-business applications run in Azure and are accessed from within Azure or from on-premises resources.
An internal Load Balancer enables the following types of load balancing:
* Within a virtual network: Load balancing from VMs in the virtual network to a set of VMs that reside within the same virtual network.
* For a cross-premises virtual network: Load balancing from on-premises computers to a set of VMs that reside within the same virtual network.
* For multi-tier applications: Load balancing for internet-facing multi-tier applications where the backend tiers are not internet-facing. The backend tiers require traffic load-balancing from the internet-facing tier (see the next figure).
* For line-of-business applications: Load balancing for line-of-business applications that are hosted in Azure without additional load balancer hardware or software. This scenario includes on-premises servers that are in the set of computers whose traffic is load-balanced.
￼

Persistence specifies the traffic from a client should be handled by the same VM for the duration of

connection.

Now, the reason you would use session persistence is typically related to the application itself.
Some applications are stateful, some are stateless.
A stateless app doesn't care where you're connecting.
A stateful application may want you to remain on a specific web server for the duration of your session.
If we were to select the session persistence dropdown here, we could configure client IP or client
IP and protocol.
I'm not worried about session persistence here, so we'll leave this set to none.
The idle timeout slider allows us to configure how long a connection should be kept open without relying
on keepalives coming from the client itself.
If we hover over the icon here, it essentially tells us the same.
Now, the last option here for the load balancer rule settings is floating IP or direct server return.
Now, if we hover over the icon here for floating IP, we can see that Microsoft recommends this feature
only when configuring the SQL always on availability Group Listener.
Now what?
Floating IP or direct server return does is allow the servers behind the load balancer to actually respond
directly to clients as part of the communication path.
What this does is relieve the load balancer of the need to handle that return traffic.
Now, like I said, Microsoft recommends this only for SQL related deployments, because what happens
is sometimes the requests coming in to the load balancer can be rather small.
But for example, if we get a request from an application for some data in a database, that request
comes in rather small, but it might pull out a ton of data.
So rather than allow the load balancer to become a bottleneck with DSR, what this does is allow that
SQL Server on the back end to actually respond directly to the client requesting the data.
We're not doing anything with SQL here, and we don't care about any kind of large data because we have
two basic Web servers.
So we're going to leave this off.
And then we can go ahead and click okay.
Once this rule updates, we'll log in to a VM on our on prem environment and see if we can get to our
load balancer internally.
Because remember, we can't get to this load balancer from outside.
It's an internal load balancer.
There's no public IP address associated with it.
So let's go ahead and refresh here.
And we can see my load balancer rule is now complete.
Now, before we test our load balancer, I just wanted to point out that if we wanted to do any kind
of inbound natting, we could select inbound Nat rules and create rules that allow us to do some inbound
Natting We're not doing any natting here, so we'll leave this alone.

the first time around, we're going to select public.

Now we're going to do this because I want to show you the interface that you're going to see.
If you decide in a production environment to deploy a standard load balancer.
Not to mention you're probably going to be asked about it on the exam.
So we've selected the standard skew here.
Now, if you notice, if I bounce back to basic, the options change.
So let's bounce back over to standard here.
And what we'll do is we'll allow Azure to create a new public IP address for us.
And what we'll do is we'll call it public IP.
My public lbw now because we selected the standard skew.
We have an additional option here for our availability.
If we select the dropdown here, we have three availability zone options.
Zone redundant one, 2 or 3.
Now, in the context of availability zones, a load balancer can be described as zone redundant or zonal
zone redundant, as you see here in the dropdown means that there are always multiple zones supporting
the load balancer.
What this means is that a single front end IP address that's configured on the load balancer can survive
a zone failure and can still be used to reach all back end pool members regardless of the zone.
Essentially what zone redundancy means is that all inbound or outbound flows are served by multiple
availability zones in a region simultaneously using that single IP address.
Now, in addition to the zone redundant option, we can also lock down the front end IP to a specific
availability zone as denoted by the one, two and three values.
What this does is guarantee a front end to a specific zone.
This is actually called a zonal front end.
In these scenarios, any inbound or outbound flow is served by a single zone in a region.
Essentially, the front end of the load balancer is going to share the same fate with the health of
whatever zone you specify here.
Now, what we're going to do here is select zone redundant.
We can go ahead and click next for tags.
We're not going to do any tagging here, so we'll review.
Make sure everything looks good and we'll go ahead and click Create.
Now, what this will do is deploy our standard public load balancer.
In the next lesson, we'll go ahead and load balance our Web oh one and Web oh two IIS servers publicly.
