HANDS-ON AZURE LAB SIMULATION: Deploy an Internal Load Balancer in Azure
Lab Overview
In this lab, you’ll learn how to deploy and configure an internal load balancer in Microsoft Azure.
An internal load balancer allows you to distribute network traffic among multiple virtual machines within a private network. This ensures high availability, fault tolerance, and scalability for your internal applications.
You’ll go through the process of configuring frontend and backend pools, setting up inbound rules, and creating health probes to monitor your virtual machines.
By the end of the lab, you will have a fully functional internal load balancer that can manage traffic across your backend servers.
Learning Objectives
* Understand how to deploy an internal load balancer in Azure.
* Learn how to configure frontend IP settings and static IPs for traffic routing.
* Gain hands-on experience setting up backend pools to distribute traffic to virtual machines.
* Configure inbound load balancing rules for HTTP traffic.
* Set up health probes to monitor the status of backend servers.
* Review and manage load balancer settings, including frontend IPs, backend pools, health probes, and load balancing rules.
To Access the Lab:
Ensure that you're logged into labITpro with the free "Azure Administrator Lab Series" subscription you setup in "Course Setup" earlier in this course, and then click here to access the lab.



120. Monitoring and Diagnostics with Network Watcher
Monitoring and Diagnostics with Network Watcher
Network Watcher is used to monitor and diagnose networking issues in an Azure environment. With it, you can gain access to real-time performance information and investigate in detail for better diagnoses. The suite of Network Watcher tools offers you the ability to diagnose common VPN Gateway and Connections issues - and it allows you to identify underlying issues.
In this section, we are going to cover the monitoring and diagnostics tools that are offered through Network Watcher. You'll see demonstrations of many of the tools that you can expect to be tested on when you sit the AZ-103 exam.

Monitoring Tools
Topology
The topology capability enables you to generate a visual diagram of the resources in a virtual network, and the relationships between the resources.
Connection Monitor
The connection monitor capability monitors communication at a regular interval and informs you of reachability, latency, and network topology changes between the VM and the endpoint.
Network Performance Monitor
Network performance monitor is a cloud-based hybrid network monitoring solution that helps you monitor network performance between various points in your network infrastructure.

Diagnostic Tools
IP Flow Verify
The IP flow verify capability enables you to specify a source and destination IPv4 address, port, protocol (TCP or UDP), and traffic direction (inbound or outbound). IP flow verify then tests the communication and informs you if the connection succeeds or fails.
Next Hop
The next hop capability enables you to specify a source and destination IPv4 address. Next hop then tests the communication and informs you what type of next hop is used to route the traffic.
Effective Security Rules
The effective security rules for a network interface are a combination of all security rules applied to the network interface, and the subnet the network interface is in.
VPN Troubleshoot
The VPN troubleshoot capability provides the ability to diagnose gateways and connections. VPN diagnostics diagnoses the health of the gateway, or gateway connection, and informs you whether a gateway and gateway connections, are available. If the gateway or connection is not available, VPN diagnostics tells you why, so you can resolve the problem.
Packet Capture
Advanced filtering options and fine-tuned controls, such as the ability to set time and size limitations, provide versatility. The capture can be stored in Azure Storage, on the VM's disk, or both. You can then analyze the capture file using several standard network capture analysis tools.
Connection Troubleshoot
The connection troubleshoot capability enables you to test a connection between a VM and another VM, an FQDN, a URI, or an IPv4 address. The test returns similar information returned when using the connection monitor capability, but tests the connection at a point in time, rather than monitoring it over time, as connection monitor does.

Metrics
Usage and Quotas
Provides a summary of how many of each network resource you have deployed in a subscription and region, and what the limit is for the resource.

Logs
NSG Flow Logs
The NSG flow log capability allows you to log the source and destination IP address, port, protocol, and whether traffic was allowed or denied by an NSG.
Diagnostic Logs
The Diagnostic logs capability provides a single interface to enable and disable network resource diagnostic logs for any existing network resource that generates a diagnostic log.
Traffic Analytics
Traffic analytics provides rich visualizations of data written to NSG flow logs.

For a complete overview of the Network Watcher suite of tools, visit this link.

So if our arm VM blew up and we deleted it, we wouldn't be able to use this option to replace that
VM because there's nothing to replace.
Now what Azure backup is going to do here is it's going to take a snapshot of this existing VM before
it replaces the disk and this is going to happen automatically.
What's going to happen is that our snapshot is going to be stored in this staging location during the
restore process.
The existing disks that are connected to the VM are going to be replaced using our restore point that
we've chosen.
The snapshot that Azure takes during this process of the existing VM is going to be copied to the vault
and retained in accordance with our retention policies for the vault.
So let's go ahead and begin the restore here.
You'll notice in the restore type for replace existing, we only have one option, and that's to replace
disks because that's what the process does.
And of course, the staging location is where we need to store our snapshot.
So we'll dump it into our exam diag storage account and then we can go ahead and click okay.
Once we click Restore here, we're going to begin the restore process of our virtual machine.

Now, what we can also see is the backup occurring for the same VM.
Now you'll notice these happened at the same time.
What this is, is the restore going to replace the existing VM.
The backup is going to be the snapshot that Azure is taking of that VM before it replaces it with our
restore.
So we'll let this run and then we'll come back in a little bit when this process has completed.

134. Protecting an Azure VM with ASR
Welcome back.
In this lesson, we're going to leverage Azure site recovery to replicate an existing Azure VM from
one region to another.
You can use Azure site recovery to do a lot of different things, including replicating on prem machines
into Azure, or you can replicate existing Azure VMs to another region.
For this demonstration here, we're going to do that replication of an Azure VM from one region to another.
Now to prepare for this demonstration, I've deployed a second recovery services vault.
If you remember early on we deployed a recovery vault called My Recovery Vault, and we deployed that
vault into the central US region.
Now because we're going to try and replicate a VM from the central US region to another region.
We can't use a recovery vault that exists within the central US region.
We also can't use a recovery services vault that's in a resource group that resides within the central
US region.
And the reason for this is simple.
If we're using Azure site recovery to protect against a site failure, what's the point of deploying
a solution that replicates a VM from one region to the same region?
So what ACR requires is that we deploy a recovery services vault into a resource group that's inside
of a separate region altogether.
So if we bounce back out to our dashboard, what I've done is deploy a recovery services vault just
like we did with the recovery vault.
And what I did is I deployed this.
Vault, which is called my Azure vault into the east US location.
I deployed it into a resource group called Dr. Site.
The Dr. Site Resource Group is also deployed in the East US region.
Now to configure our ACR to protect an existing Azure VM, what we're going to do here is from our Azure
Vault Select Site Recovery.
Now since we're protecting an Azure VM, we don't need to prepare any type of infrastructure.
However, if we were protecting an on prem machine, be it a Hyper-V machine, a VM machine or even
a physical machine, we'd select prepare infrastructure.
We would tell ASR that our machines are on prem and then we would specify where we're replicating to
and how our VMs are virtualized or not virtualized at all.
We're going to close this because instead we're going to replicate an existing VM from Azure to another
region in Azure.
So we'll go ahead and under for on Prem Machines and Azure VMs, we'll select step one, which is to
replicate application.
What we need to do here is complete three steps.
We need to configure the source.
We need to select the virtual machines we want to replicate, and then we have to configure the replication
settings.
So from here we're going to tell Azure site Recovery that the source of our VM is in Azure, and the
machine that we want to replicate is in the central US region.
We have a choice of resource manager or classic deployment models.
All of our VMs are deployed using the resource manager deployment model.
And of course we're replicating from our pay as you go subscription.
The VM that we want to replicate is in the My second org resource group.
Remember, our VM was moved from our AZ 103 exam resource group to my second org resource group as part
of an earlier exercise.
So what we can do here is select.
Okay.
And then what this is going to do is enumerate any VMs that are available to replicate and we can see
here our VM is available.
So we'll go ahead and select our VM and we can see that we have one selected virtual machine now and
we can click okay.
At this point we need to specify the target location.
We're going to target east us as our location.
Our target subscription is going to remain our pay as you go.
And we can see here that by default, site recovery is going to mirror the source site configuration
to the target site.
It's going to create and use any necessary resource groups, storage accounts, virtual networks, all
that stuff to mirror the source environment to the destination.
If we want to change anything, we can click customize here and then we can change whatever settings
we want to change.
We're not going to change anything here, so we'll go back and we can then customize a replication policy.
The default name here is 24.
Our retention policy.
We have a recovery point retention of 24 hours with an app, consistent snapshot frequency of four hours.
If we click Customize here, we can change these policy settings.
You'll see here that we can enable multi VM consistency by creating a new replication group.
Now what a replication group is, is a setting that allows you to replicate specific virtual machines
together.
A good use case for this would be a situation where you have an application such as Great Plains that
includes a VM that runs the application, a database server that runs the database, and maybe some
other ancillary servers that need to come over as a group.
So what you would do is you would add all of those servers to that particular replication group, so
they all replicate at the same time to maintain a consistency for the application.
We're only replicating one VM, so we don't need multi VM consistency here.
So we'll close this window here and then we can also show details for extension settings.
If we click show details here, we can allow site recovery to manage updating our settings.
If we hover over the icon here, we can see that site recovery manage extensions on all replicated items
that are associated with the vault and what it does, it keeps them up to date.
However, we also have the option to update these extensions manually by changing this in the dropdown
box.
Generally speaking, you allow Azure to handle these tasks so we can see what's going to happen here
when we configure this replication that a new target resource group is going to be created in the East
US region called my second ARG Dash ACR.
We're going to get a new cache storage account.
We don't have any availability sets being created because we don't have one deployed in our source.
A new target, virtual network is going to be deployed.
And we're going to have two standard disks deployed as well.
If we hover over the icon here for Target virtual Network, we can see that this is the virtual network
to which the failed over VM is going to be associated with.

deployed.

And what this is going to do is initiate the deployment.
We can see that our deployments are successful.
Let's close these out so we can see we have green check boxes down the line here for source VMs and
settings.
So we'll go ahead and enable replication.
And we can see here that the operation's in progress.
If we go down here into replicated items, eventually our protected items here will show up.

VM.

135. Failing Over with ASR
Welcome back.
So now that our VM has replicated, what we're going to do is perform a test failover to ensure that
the failover would work to perform a test failover, We can select our VM from the replicated items
list and then we can either select test failover or failover for real.
What we're going to do here for this demonstration is perform a test failover.
So let's go ahead and click test failover.
And from the test failover screen, we need to select a recovery point that we want to failover from
now.
You'll see here we have four options latest, which is the lowest RPO latest processed, which is a
low to the latest app, consistent or custom.
If we hover over the icon here, we can see that if we choose the latest option, the failover is going
to be triggered after processing all of the data sent to the service.
Now, the time that it takes is going to depend on the amount of unprocessed logs.
So if this is a large server doing a lot of stuff, it could take a while.
What we'll do here is we'll choose latest.

We don't have a production network here, so we can go ahead and fail over like we plan on doing.

137. Create an Action Group by Using the Azure Portal
Welcome back.
In this lesson, I want to cover action groups.
Now, what an action group is, is it's actually a collection of notification preferences.
Now, these notification preferences or this collection of notification preferences, I should say,
is defined by the owner of an Azure subscription, which is me, Azure Monitor and Service health alerts
use action groups to notify users whenever an alert has been triggered.
For example, you may have a systems admin group that needs to be alerted when certain things happen
in the environment.
This is where action groups come into play.
You can configure up to 2000 different action groups within a subscription.
What we're going to do here is create an action group.
Now before we create our action group, I just wanted to point out that each action group consists of
a name, an action type, and the details.
The name is obviously what you want to call that action group, and it needs to be unique within the
action group.
The action type is what action is being performed.
For example, you may want to alert your users by sending a voice mail or an email or even triggering
certain automated actions.
And then the details include the corresponding details that are going to vary depending on what the
action type is.
So to create our action group through the Azure portal, what I'm going to do here is browse over to
monitor.
If I select alerts.
I can go down and I can select from here the manage actions option.
And that's over here at the top of the page.
So go ahead and click manage actions here.
From the Manage Actions page is where we actually create our action group.
So we'll go ahead and select the Add action group option here.
And then this is where we can define our action group.
So we'll give our action group a name.
We'll just call it my group for now, and we'll call it my action group.
Now, the short name here, what this is, is this is included in email and SMS notifications to identify
which action group was the source of the notification.
So we'll just duplicate our name here and then we can see that the subscription that we're working in
is the AZ 103.
If we hover over the icon here, it basically tells us this is where you're going to choose the subscription
in which the group is going to be saved.
Now, as far as the resource group goes, this is the resource group that this group is going to be
associated with.
If you select the dropdown here.
You can select an existing resource group or let it create a new one.
I'll add this to my resource group here.
Now, once I've configured the information up here for my action group, I need to define the actions.
We also have to define the action type.
So what I'm going to do here, I'll just call it my action.
And the action type will use email.
And when I do that, it's going to ask me for more information about my action type.
So I'm going to leave the name at my action and I'll choose email as the option.
And then this email address is going to be the email that the action goes to,
and I'll send it to myself here.
Now, what you would typically do, for example, in the the example I gave earlier where you wanted
to send notifications to a group of system administrators, you could have a distribution list with
an email address that contains all of those sysadmins and then you would put that email address of the
DLL here.
Now, we're not going to do any other actions here, but down here it mentions the option to enable
the Common Alert schema.
Now, if I select the Common Alert schema, what this does is give me the advantage of having a single
extensible and unified alert payload.
If I select Learn more here, we can see that the Common Alert schema, what it does is it standardizes
the consumption experience for alert notifications.
And basically if you read all of this jargon here, what it's telling you is, is it standardizes the
alert schema that it sends out.
So instead of reading this all to you, what I'll do is just point out the URL here where you can go

We're not going to use it here, so let me bounce back over and just leave this set to no and then click.
Okay.
Now, if we can see here, we get a bullet here saying that the Max character is 12 for the short name.
And I thought I was within 12 here, two, four, six, eight, ten.
I was 13, so I'll just call it action group.
I was one character over.
So there we go.
Action group.
The bullet goes away and we can click okay.
So now that our action group has defined what I should point out is that emails that are sent to this
